DRAFT
Climate Action Plan Update 
ES-1 
Executive Summary 
The City of Oceanside (City) is situated in the northwest corner of San Diego County and 
embodies a character that is unique to the region. With Camp Pendleton to the north, the Pacific 
Ocean to west, and the distinctly divergent cities of Vista and Carlsbad to the east and south, 
respectively; Oceanside stands out as a unique coastal community that maintains a working-
class character while offering tourism and economic opportunities. Oceanside functions as both 
a suburb for regional employment centers, and its own economic hub of tourism, retail and 
restaurants, light manufacturing, and biotechnology sectors, to name a few. The developable 
land area of Oceanside is largely built out; however, the future holds tremendous opportunities 
for creating connectivity and efficiency in land uses, transportation, and energy systems.  
Oceanside, like its neighboring cities, is already being affected by 
climate change. The City’s coastal location presents additional 
challenges resulting from increased sea-level rise and associated 
coastal hazards such as beach erosion, and water quality and habitat 
degradation. While climate change is a global issue, steps can be 
taken to mitigate the effects of climate change at the regional and 
local level by implementing actions to reduce greenhouse gas (GHG) 
emissions locally. The San Diego Association of Governments 
(SANDAG) leads the Regional Climate Action Planning Framework (ReCAP) to provide a framework 
for individual jurisdictions within the San Diego region to develop their own Climate Action Plans 
(CAPs). By establishing a regional framework, ReCAP promotes a consistent approach to emissions 
reduction across the entire region, ultimately contributing to the statewide goals. The City of 
Oceanside recognizes its role in the regional effort and has prepared this Climate Action Plan 
Update (CAP Update) to outline its strategies and commitments for reducing GHG emissions while 
prioritizing quality of life and economic development in Oceanside. 
ReCAP identifies best 
practices and guidance 
for preparing Climate 
Action Plans (CAPs) for 
the San Diego region and 
assists in monitoring 
implementation of CAPs. 
Sprinter light rail 


DRAFT
Oceanside General Plan Update 
ES-2 
While the City’s Onward Oceanside General Plan Update focuses on 
infill development in priority areas, the CAP Update builds on the 
success of the City’s 2019 CAP which resulted in several key actions 
for Oceanside such as joining the Clean Energy Alliance (CEA), 
installation of renewable energy facilities, adoption of the Zero 
Waste Plan 2020 Update, among others.  
This CAP Update serves to consolidate the General Plan Update 
goals with ongoing sustainability initiatives to modernize 
transportation systems, development patterns, and buildings such 
that future growth in Oceanside is both sustainable and cost-
effective for community members.  
Components of the CAP Update 
The CAP Update consists of several components including a GHG 
emissions analysis, GHG reduction targets, GHG reduction 
measures and associated actions, and procedures for monitoring 
and reporting progress on the implementation of GHG reduction 
measures. Figure ES-1 provides a graphical summary of the CAP 
Update components which are described in the sections below. 
These core components of the CAP Update allow for data-based 
policy decisions that are tailored to the community character and 
priorities of Oceanside. 
 
The CAP Update 
integrates the City’s past 
and current GHG 
reduction efforts with 
additional measures that 
seek to balance GHG 
reduction with other 
priorities, including 
quality of life, economic 
development, and fiscal 
responsibility.

Greenhouse Gas Emissions Analysis 
The first step in the GHG emissions for the CAP Update includes developing a communitywide 
GHG emissions inventory to reflect baseline conditions in the City. This is followed by a forecast 
of emissions for select milestone years to study the trajectory of emissions associated with 
anticipated growth in the City. The baseline inventory and forecasts provide an understanding 
of the GHG emissions generating activities that occur in Oceanside and how they are expected to 
change in the future with the impacts of policies and legislation already in place. The GHG 
analysis also provides insight into policy levers that may affect GHG emissions and where 
mitigation efforts can be more impactful.  
The results of the GHG emissions analysis are summarized below:  
• 
Activities in the City generated approximately 846,000 metric tons of carbon dioxide 
equivalent (MTCO2e) in 2018. The term CO2e accounts for contributions from carbon 
dioxide, methane and nitrous oxide based on their varying global warming potentials. 


DRAFT
Oceanside General Plan Update 
ES-4 
o 
The largest contributor to total emissions was fuel combustion in on-road vehicles.  
o 
The next two largest emissions sources were grid-purchased electricity and natural 
gas use in buildings and facilities within the City. 
• 
Without any future actions (i.e., under “business-as-usual” conditions, also known as 
BAU forecast), GHG emissions are expected to increase through 2050. However, with an 
accounting of the impact of the City joining CEA and additional state, and federal actions 
(i.e., under “legislative adjusted business-as-usual” conditions, also known as ABAU 
forecast), emissions are expected to decline from 2018 levels through 2050. 
o 
The primary drivers of emissions reductions from local, state, and federal 
interventions are increased grid-supplied renewable electricity from joining CEA, 
implementation of the renewables portfolio standard through Senate Bill (SB) 100 and 
SB 1020, as well as increased zero-emission vehicle penetration from Advanced Clean 
Cars II regulations. 
Greenhouse Gas Reduction Targets and Gap 
The GHG reduction targets of the CAP Update for the years 2030 and 2045 align with State's GHG 
emission reduction targets established in SB 32 and Assembly Bill (AB) 1279. The City’s GHG 
reduction target for the year 2050 aligns with the General Plan Update horizon year. These 
targets are updated from the City’s 2019 CAP to incorporate long-term GHG reduction targets 
for 2045 and 2050, and to realign with the more recent 2018 GHG emissions inventory. The City’s 
GHG reduction targets in this CAP Update are: 
• 
Reducing emissions 27 percent below 2018 levels by 2030 (aligned with SB 32),  
• 
Reducing emissions 90 percent below 2018 levels by 2045 (aligned with AB 1279), and 
• 
Maintaining emissions 90 percent below 2018 levels through 2050 (General Plan Update 
horizon year). 
These GHG reduction targets are not expected to be met without additional action beyond 
planned local, State, and federal interventions. Table ES-1 and Figure ES-2 provide a summary 
of the ABAU forecast in comparison with the updated GHG reduction targets, and the emissions 
gap that would need to be reduced to meet the targets. The GHG reduction measures and actions 
included in this CAP Update were developed to address this emissions gap through local action.

however, the 2045 and 2050 targets are not demonstrated 
to be met with the current measures alone. This CAP 
Update lays the foundation for the deep decarbonization 
needed to align with the State’s GHG reduction priorities.

will provide the feedback loop to inform and refine 
longer-term GHG reduction initiatives as technology cost

ES-7 
 
 
Implementation and Monitoring  
The CAP Update sets monitoring procedures for measuring progress as GHG reduction measures 
and actions are implemented over time. City staff will annually evaluate the implementation 
status of each action by monitoring Key Performance Indicators. GHG emissions inventory 
updates will be completed biennially, and a monitoring report will be prepared annually which 
will be used to communicate progress and identify actions that need to be improved, adjusted, 
or replaced. Finally, the City will prepare an update to the CAP every five years. 
The CAP Update has been prepared as a “qualified” CAP consistent with the California 
Environmental Quality Act (CEQA) guidelines. Under the provisions of a qualified CAP, if a 
development project in Oceanside aligns with the City's goals and reduces emissions consistent 
with the CAP Update, it can streamline the analysis of its GHG emissions under CEQA. A 
consistency checklist will be created within a year of CAP adoption to help projects show 
consistency with the CAP's goals. 
 
 
Measure Highlights 
• 
Increase renewable energy availability by providing 100% renewable electricity through the 
Clean Energy Alliance (CEA) by 2030 with a 95% participation rate.  
• 
Increase behind-the-meter solar photovoltaic (PV) capacity in the City to 125 megawatts 
(MW) by 2030 and 165 MW by 2045. 
• 
Increase active transportation infrastructure to provide safe alternative options to vehicle 
travel, installing or upgrading 26 miles of bike lanes/paths by 2030 and 50 miles by 2045. 
• 
Plant 200 trees each year citywide, to achieve 1,200 net new trees planted by 2030 and 4,200 
by 2045. 
• 
Sustain local growth through providing green job opportunities to residents and promoting 
local businesses.

DRAFT
Climate Action Plan Update 
1-1 
1 
Introduction 
The City of Oceanside (City) is committed to providing a high quality of life for the community 
while supporting sustainable land use patterns, healthy living, economic development, and 
community character. By using energy more efficiently, harnessing renewable energy to power 
buildings, reducing, reusing, recycling, and composting waste, conserving water, and enhancing 
access to sustainable transportation modes, the City can invest local revenue in its economy, 
create new green jobs, and improve residents’ quality of life in sustainable ways. To that end, the 
City has developed and implemented a Climate Action Plan (CAP) that integrates the City’s past 
and current greenhouse gas (GHG) reduction efforts with additional measures that seek to 
balance emissions reductions with other priorities, including quality of life, housing, 
employment, and fiscal responsibility.  
The City first adopted a CAP in 2019. To inform this CAP Update, the City has assessed its 
progress in implementing GHG emissions reduction measures outlined in the 2019 CAP, 
subsequent regional policy initiatives related to climate mitigation, and new state legislation 
that establishes and refines the state’s expectations for local climate action. The City has sought 
to prepare an updated CAP that 1) leverages regional climate mitigation efforts, 2) aligns with 
state emissions reduction targets, and 3) can be effectively implemented without extensive 
reallocation of City resources. 
1.1 Climate Action Plan Overview 
A CAP is a planning document that outlines measures and actions a jurisdiction will implement to 
address the threat of climate change through reduction of local GHG emissions. Development of a 
CAP is a data-based process in which jurisdictions evaluate the volume of GHGs emitted by 
activities within their boundary, such as energy use, waste generation, and vehicle travel, during a 
baseline year and set a future target for reducing GHG emissions through policies, programs, and 
incentives. The success of a CAP requires action at all levels 
of a community, where the role of a local government is to 
support community members in reducing the carbon 
footprint of daily activities. These governmental actions can 
take 
many 
forms, 
such 
as 
providing 
educational 
opportunities, adopting codes that support low-carbon 
buildings and transportation options, and decarbonizing 
essential services.  
Climate Action Plans serve as a 
policy framework for reducing 
GHG emissions at the local 
level while maximizing 
community benefit. 


DRAFT
Oceanside General Plan Update 
1-2 
CAPs developed by local governments in California are typically aligned with California’s goals 
and strategies for reducing statewide GHG emissions. Since 2006, the State has adopted near- 
and long-term GHG reduction targets through legislation that align with the best available 
climate science and global climate stabilization efforts. More recently, the Paris Agreement of 
2015 set long-term goals for all nations to substantially reduce GHG emissions to hold global 
temperature increase to well below 2°C (35.6°F) above pre-industrial levels and pursue efforts to 
limit it to 1.5°C (34.7°F) above pre-industrial levels. In order to 
meet these climate stabilization goals, global net-zero 
emissions would need to be achieved by 2050, in which all GHG 
emissions sources are balanced by carbon removal (United 
Nations ND). California established a trajectory for meeting 
this target when Assembly Bill (AB) 1279 was signed into law 
in 2022, setting a statewide target for reaching net-zero 
emissions by 2045. Local jurisdictions are encouraged by the 
State to develop strategies for reducing their fair share of 
emissions in support of limiting the impacts of climate change 
in the world’s seventh largest economy.  
The City recognizes the importance of swift and concerted action to address the challenge of 
climate change, such that residents, workers, and visitors are protected from the worst impacts of 
this phenomenon. The CAP, and this subsequent update, is a key strategy document that strives to 
reduce the City’s contribution to climate change in a manner that supports a healthy, connected, 
and economically prosperous community.  
1.2 2019 Climate Action Plan 
In April 2019, the City Council adopted Oceanside’s first CAP, which was prepared with Phase 1 
of the General Plan Update (GPU). The 2019 CAP sought to align local emission reduction efforts 
with state legislation while balancing community interests. It laid out the framework for how the 
City would address its fair share of GHG emissions while providing resources for residents, 
business owners, and others in the community to reduce their own emissions. 
The 2019 CAP’s emission reduction targets were developed to align with the adopted state 
legislation at the time, including AB 32, Senate Bill (SB) 32, and Executive Order S-03-05. 
Implementation of this CAP’s measures resulted in several milestones for the City in addressing 
climate change. These actions include: 
gO’side Electric Shuttle

Community Plan. 
 
1.3 2024 Climate Action Plan Update 
This CAP Update builds upon the goals and successes of the 2019 CAP, is based on a more recent 
GHG emissions analysis, and reflects state legislation adopted since the City’s 2019 CAP was 
developed. The City has prepared the CAP Update as part of the second phase of a comprehensive 
GPU. Through this update, the City is upholding its commitment to enhance the quality of life in 
Oceanside to the highest standards through sustainable land use patterns, resources that 
promote healthy living, and enhancement of the visual character of the community. The CAP 
Update outlines a variety of strategies and measures designed to mitigate the anticipated GHG 
emissions impacts of future development and reduce local GHG emissions in accordance with 
state goals by addressing energy efficiency, renewable energy, low and zero-emission 
transportation options, water conservation, solid waste management, and urban forestry. 
Electric Vehicle Charging on North Horne St.

1-4 
CAP UPDATE PURPOSE AND OBJECTIVES 
The CAP Update represents an important step in addressing global climate change and its effects 
on the City. The CAP Update intends to attain alignment with state legislation and directives and to 
continuously improve the City’s climate action efforts through fine-tuning emissions reduction 
measures by drawing from past achievements, leveraging federal, state, and regional support, and 
adopting the latest best practices and new technology. The goals of the CAP encompass a 
comprehensive approach to addressing climate change including the following objectives: 
• 
Aligning local efforts with state climate strategies; 
• 
Achieving climate stabilization;  
• 
Fostering economic stability;  
• 
Creating opportunities for employment in emerging economic sectors; 
• 
Realizing cost savings with a positive return on investment;  
• 
Promoting equity; and  
• 
Generating environmental co-benefits.  
With the implementation of the CAP Update, the City seeks to generate a range of 
environmental, economic, and public health co-benefits like improved public health through 
active transportation and clean energy initiatives resulting in cleaner air, the preservation of 
Oceanside’s 
valuable 
tree 
canopy, 
and 
improving the green jobs market. The CAP 
Update measures strive for farmland and 
habitat conservation resulting in positive 
outcomes 
like 
safeguarding 
essential 
ecosystems and promoting biodiversity. These 
efforts contribute to enhancing visual quality, 
making Oceanside more enjoyable for residents 
and more appealing to tourists. Through a 
multifaceted strategy, the CAP Update strives to 
create a sustainable and just future for all 
community members. 
Garrison Creek Trail  


DRAFT
Climate Action Plan Update 
1-5 
SCOPE OF THE CAP UPDATE 
The CAP Update focuses on efforts the City can take to reduce GHG emissions that result from 
activities that occur within its geographic boundary and over which the City can exert some 
influence. As such, some GHG emissions are excluded from the scope of the CAP Update as they 
cannot be directly controlled or influenced through local government actions. Some excluded 
GHG emissions sources include marine vessel fuel use, agriculture, and use of consumer 
products, as these emissions sources are outside of the City’s authority to regulate. However, the 
City recognizes that consumer choices have an impact on the environment and through this CAP 
Update will encourage residents and visitors to prioritize locally produced foods and products 
while supporting local small businesses.  
CAP UPDATE ELEMENTS 
The CAP Update includes four key components that create a holistic strategy for the City to 
address climate change. Generally, these include a baseline GHG emissions analysis, setting GHG 
reduction targets, developing GHG reduction measures and the associated actions for 
implementation, and procedures for monitoring and reporting progress on the implementation 
of GHG reduction measures. Figure 1-1 provides a summary of the elements and process for 
development of the CAP Update.  
 
Figure 1-1 
Components of the Climate Action Plan Development Process 
Greenhouse Gas Emissions Analysis 
The initial evaluation of GHG emissions is called a GHG emissions inventory, which establishes 
an understanding of how GHG emissions are generated within a physical boundary such that 
policies can be developed to address individual GHG emissions sources. After a GHG inventory is 
completed, a forecast of future GHG emissions is developed which provides insight into how GHG 
emissions may change with growth and how external factors, such as state and federal 
legislation, may influence how GHG emissions evolve. As part of the CAP Update, the City 


DRAFT
Oceanside General Plan Update 
1-6 
completed a 2018 communitywide GHG emissions inventory to establish a baseline for 
developing a GHG emissions forecast and setting GHG reduction targets. A GHG emissions 
forecast was developed which evaluates how GHG emissions may change in the future under two 
scenarios. The first scenario is a business-as-usual scenario, which looks at how growth alone 
would influence future GHG emissions. The second scenario is a legislative adjusted business-
as-usual scenario, which provides an understanding of how currently adopted state and federal 
legislation may affect future GHG emissions, as well as the impact of the City joining CEA. Clean 
Energy Alliance (CEA) is a community choice energy (CCE) provider that allows Oceanside to 
purchase power to meet the community’s electricity needs, offering an alternative to San Diego 
Gas & Electric. This analysis sets the stage for areas where GHG reduction efforts by the City 
should be focused to synchronize with state and federal regulations.  
Greenhouse Gas Reduction Targets 
Another key component of a CAP is setting GHG reduction targets that guide how aggressive 
GHG reduction policies should be. The GHG reduction targets of the CAP Update align with 
statewide legislative adopted targets, including Senate Bill (SB) 32 and Assembly Bill (AB) 1279. 
The CAP Update has developed new GHG reduction targets from those adopted with the 2019 
CAP, such that the targets can be evaluated from the new 2018 baseline GHG emissions inventory 
and follow the most recent state guidance. This results in two GHG reduction targets for the years 
2030 and 2045, which are evaluated based on a reduction below 2018 communitywide GHG 
emissions levels. An additional long-term target for the year 2050 is considered in the CAP 
Update, which aligns with the General Plan Update horizon year. The GHG reduction targets 
included in the CAP Update are: 
• 
Reducing emissions 27 percent below 2018 levels by 2030 (aligned with SB 32),  
• 
Reducing emissions 90 percent below 2018 levels by 2045 (aligned with AB 1279), and 
• 
Maintaining emissions 90 percent below 2018 levels through 2050 (General Plan Update 
horizon year). 
Greenhouse Gas Reduction Measures and Actions 
The CAP Update includes a suite of policies focused on reducing local GHG emissions, which are 
called GHG reduction measures. Each GHG reduction measure is focused on reducing emissions 
from specific sources under the City’s control, such as on-road transportation. Measures are 
implemented through a discrete set of actions.  


DRAFT
Climate Action Plan Update 
1-7 
The core GHG reduction measures focus on increasing 
the availability of renewable energy, enhancing energy 
efficiency, 
providing 
alternative 
modes 
of 
transportation, supporting a transition to zero-emission 
vehicles and equipment, improving water efficiency, and 
supporting increased carbon sequestration in the urban 
forest, natural lands, and working lands. In total, the 
CAP Update includes 17 GHG reduction measures with 
120 implementation actions to achieve the GHG 
reduction targets.  
Implementation Monitoring and Reporting 
Understanding the effectiveness of GHG reduction measures and enabling flexibility to make 
data-informed decisions about if and when measures should be modified is key to achieving long-
term GHG reduction targets. The CAP Update sets monitoring procedures for measuring progress 
as GHG reduction measures and actions are implemented over time. To appropriately allocate 
resources to programs and policies, the City must execute actions on their intended timeline and 
determine how effective they are at achieving the intended results. Additionally, the CAP Update 
includes procedures for reporting implementation progress to the public and decision-makers 
such that the City can demonstrate fulfillment of the commitments made in the CAP Update.  
CAP UPDATE PROCESS 
In early 2020, the City began the simultaneous process of 
completing its GPU, creating a Smart and Sustainable 
Corridors Specific Plan, and developing the Draft South 
Morro Hills Community Plan. In early 2023, the City kicked 
off the CAP Update as a companion to these efforts, in 
which the CAP Update is closely tied to future buildout 
assumptions and policy initiatives. The City completed the 
CAP Update’s GHG emissions analysis in fall of 2023, which 
included preparation a GHG emissions inventory and forecast, and developing GHG reduction 
targets. From fall of 2023 to early 2024, the City developed and refined GHG emissions reduction 
measures and actions to be included in the CAP Update. Engagement with City staff, members of 
the public, government officials, and other stakeholders was conducted throughout the entirety 
of the project.  
The Climate Action Plan 
Update is closely integrated 
into Oceanside’s General Plan 
Update, and is consistent with 
goals and policies of all 
General Plan elements. 
Metrolink Commuter Train  


DRAFT
Oceanside General Plan Update 
1-8 
As part of the General Plan Update and CAP Update process, the City engaged with the 
community using various methods to understand priorities and needs. The CAP Update was 
closely tied to the GPU, with many of the GPU’s core elements being included in the CAP Update. 
As such, the extensive community engagement performed for the various GPU components was 
leveraged to incorporate community priorities into the CAP Update. Outreach conducted to 
solicit community values and priorities included numerous workshops and open houses, in both 
virtual and in-person formats, surveys, and ad hoc discussions with small groups and individual 
community members.  
In October 2023, the City issued an online survey specific to the CAP Update in both English and 
Spanish. The survey was focused on both educating community members on the CAP Update 
process, while soliciting feedback on the types of measures that would receive high participation. 
Nearly 470 community members participated in the survey. The responses to the survey were 
used in the development of the GHG reduction measures, which highlighted the importance of 
education and information sharing, ensuring requirements are cost-effective for businesses and 
residents, and considerations for potential negative secondary impacts to low-income residents 
and historically disadvantaged communities. The results of the survey are provided for reference 
in Appendix C. 
CAP UPDATE ORGANIZATION 
Chapter 2 provides background information describing the science underlying climate change 
and the impacts anticipated for the City. It also describes existing federal and State regulations 
related to GHG emissions and climate change, regional coordination including transportation 
planning, and other regional planning efforts related to the CAP. Chapter 3 includes details on 
the City’s baseline emissions inventory and projections and establishes reduction targets for

2 
Background 
The City of Oceanside (City) is committed to providing residents, workers and visitors with 
healthy and resilient environment fosters economic opportunity and prosperity. These goals 
align with climate mitigation efforts. The City acknowledges the threat of human-induced 
climate change and recognizes the community benefits that can be achieved by reducing the 
City’s contribution to this phenomenon. For example, improving energy efficiency reduces 
energy costs and, in turn, the cost of services for community members while reducing greenhouse 
gas (GHG) emissions from energy generation. Enhancing neighborhood connectivity and 
walkability fosters livable and healthy communities while reducing vehicle travel. These are only 
some examples of opportunities the City is capitalizing on as part of City programs and long-
range planning efforts.  
This chapter provides the background context for development of the Climate Action Plan (CAP) 
Update. It includes an introduction to the science behind climate change and its current and 
anticipated impacts on the City. Next is a discussion of the drivers of climate action and 
initiatives to address climate change at the federal, state and regional levels, including a 
summary of actions the City has taken that reduce its contribution to climate change. This 
chapter also discusses the intersection between the CAP Update, the in-progress General Plan 
Update, and the California Environmental Quality Act (CEQA).  
2.1 Climate Science 
The physical phenomena behind climate change contribute to the greenhouse effect, a natural 
process that insulates the Earth and helps regulate its temperature through the presence of GHGs 
in the atmosphere. The greenhouse effect is demonstrated in Figure 2-1. Metaphorically, GHGs, 
such as water vapor, carbon dioxide (CO2), methane (CH4), and nitrous oxide (N2O), act as a blanket 
surrounding our planet. This “blanket” is necessary to sustain life on Earth—without it, 
temperatures would be much colder, leaving Earth unsuitable for human life. However, even though 
the greenhouse effect is a necessary, natural process, it has been exacerbated by human activities, 
notably through the burning of fossil fuels and the removal of natural “sinks,” such as forests and 
grasslands, that serve to trap some of the GHGs that would otherwise reach the atmosphere. These 
human activities have released, and are continuing to release, an excess amount of GHGs into the 
atmosphere, causing the Earth’s climate to warm at an unprecedented rate. 


DRAFT
Oceanside General Plan Update 
2-2 
 
Source: Ascent Environmental 2021. 
Figure 2-1 
The Greenhouse Effect 
The Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) published the Sixth Assessment Report, 
a science-based summary of the most recent research on the causes and impacts of climate 
change. According to the report, there is scientific consensus that observed increases in 
atmospheric GHG concentrations and the consequential warming of Earth’s atmosphere, oceans, 
and lands have “unequivocally” been caused by human activities and influence (IPCC 2021). The 
report further states that human activities are estimated to have caused over two (2) degrees 
Fahrenheit (°F) of warming across the globe compared to pre-industrial era levels (i.e., before the 
year 1900), and the global average temperature is expected to increase by up to approximately 
8 °F by the end of the century unless additional efforts to reduce GHG emissions are made.  
As California continues to experience rising temperatures, more extreme storms, more frequent 
and intense wildfires, and intense droughts, it has become evident that the effects of climate 
change are already occurring. The impacts of climate change in California vary across the state 
due to its diverse biophysical setting, climate, and community characteristics, but even modest 
changes in temperature and precipitation patterns could have significant effects on the state’s 


DRAFT
Climate Action Plan Update 
2-3 
ecosystems. At a regional level, the average annual temperature in the San Diego region is 
projected to increase between 5 and 10 ˚F by the end of the 21st century (University of San Diego 
2023). Along the San Diego coast, sea level is expected to rise one (1) foot by 2050 with an 
increase of at least three (3) feet by 2100 due to rising temperatures across the globe and ocean 
thermal expansion coupled with melting ice (University of San Diego 2023). These changes are 
expected to result in adverse impacts on transportation infrastructure, energy systems, human 
health and safety, and economic prosperity in the region (OPR, CEC, and CNRA 2018).  
2.2 Local Climate Change Impacts 
As a coastal community with a substantial wildland-urban interface (WUI), floodplains, sloping 
terrain, farmland, and a variety of microclimates, Oceanside is exposed to a wide range of natural 
hazards that threaten public safety and the built environment. Wildfire has affected Oceanside, with 
the 2017 Lilac Fire being the most significant wildfire in recent history. The City’s highly eroded 
coastline is susceptible to wave uprush and inundation during king tides and high surf events, which 
has damaged many beachfront homes and public facilities in recent years. Properties adjacent to 
Loma Alta Creek experience flooding during major rain events, as do segments of State Route 78 in 
proximity to Buena Vista Lagoon (City of Oceanside ND). The segment of Coast Highway that 
crosses Buena Vista Lagoon can become impassable during major rain events. Droughts have 
threatened agricultural operations in the 
South Morro Hills area, where farmers 
struggle with the high cost of water. 
Landslides have damaged and destroyed 
single-family homes in Rancho del Oro and 
other neighborhoods. In short, much of 
Oceanside is vulnerable to natural hazards, 
with the City’s coastline, northern and 
eastern boundaries, hillsides, and areas 
near Loma Alta Creek and Buena Vista 
Creek and Lagoon being particularly at risk.  
Oceanside’s proximity to the Pacific Ocean coastline is core to the City’s identity. However, this 
characteristic presents additional challenges that will be exacerbated by climate change and 
associated sea level rise (SLR). Ocean water levels have already risen six inches in the last 
century, as measured in La Jolla, and SLR is expected to accelerate as climate change continues. 
Projections show that local ocean levels are expected to rise an additional two inches to one foot 
by 2030, and up to three feet by 2100 (City of Oceanside 2023). The range of SLR impacts that 
will be realized are driven by the ability to reduce global GHG emissions. 
Flooding on Highway 78 in Oceanside on January  
23, 2024 (NBC San Diego) 


DRAFT
Oceanside General Plan Update 
2-4 
The City’s efforts to reduce local GHG emissions provide a direct benefit to protecting coastal 
access, property, and critical infrastructure from the impacts of SLR. 
In addition to increased vulnerability to natural hazards, Oceanside’s coastal zone faces new 
challenges, including increasing visitor traffic, more stringent water quality and habitat 
management requirements, and aging infrastructure. In the past, natural hazards have caused 
significant damage to infrastructure and property. In the future, these vulnerabilities 
exacerbated by climate change will potentially increase as the intensity and frequency of 
hazardous events increase with climate change. In response, the City is currently updating its 
Local Coastal Program and has developed this CAP Update to reduce its contribution to climate 
change and establish a long-range vision for the community. 
2.3 State and Federal Regulatory Framework 
In response to the steady increases in GHG emissions and the threat of global climate change, 
federal and state governments have already taken several steps to reduce GHG emissions. These 
efforts, and the legislative background summarized in the following sections, provide important 
policy direction and context for this CAP Update. 
FEDERAL CLIMATE LEGISLATION 
The Infrastructure Investment and Jobs Act 
The Infrastructure Investment and Jobs Act (IIJA), also known as the Bipartisan Infrastructure 
Act, was passed by Congress on November 6, 2021 to provide a once-in-generation bipartisan 
infrastructure bill. IIJA is the largest long-term investment to rebuild roads, bridges, and 
railways; expand access to high-speed internet; tackle the climate crisis; advance environmental 
justice; and invest in underrepresented communities. By investing in transportation and public 
transit, GHG emissions will be reduced and clean, zero-emission vehicles will be incentivized.  
Corporate Average Fuel Economy Standards 
On April 1, 2022, the U.S. Department of Transportation updated its Corporate Average Fuel 
Economy (CAFE) standards to apply to mileage standards for passenger cars and light trucks. The 
standards apply to model years 2024 and 2026, requiring an industry-wide fuel economy average 
of approximately 49 miles per gallon for passenger cars and light trucks for model year 2026. The 
new standards will increase fuel efficiency eight (8) percent annually for model years 2024 to 
2025, and 10 percent for model year 2026. 


DRAFT
Climate Action Plan Update 
2-5 
Inflation Reduction Act 
The Inflation Reduction Act (IRA) was signed into 
law on August 16, 2022. It addresses climate 
pollution by investing in GHG reduction strategies. It 
will make a historic down payment on deficit 
reduction to fight inflation, invest in domestic 
energy production and manufacturing, and reduce 
carbon emissions by roughly 40 percent below 2005 
levels by 2030. It builds on the opportunities passed 
into law in IIJA by supporting projects across electric 
vehicle charging, power infrastructure and climate 
resilience. Examples include supporting U.S. manufacturing of solar panels, wind turbines, 
batteries, and critical materials by expanding production tax credits; providing direct incentives 
for American families to decarbonize their homes; and establishing the Greenhouse Gas 
Reduction Fund to support rapid deployment of low- to zero-emission technologies to benefit 
disadvantaged communities. 
Renewable Fuel Standard Program 
The Renewable Fuel Standard (RFS) program was created under the Energy Policy Act of 2005 
and further expanded by the Energy Independence and Security Act of 2007, which amended the 
Clean Air Act (CAA). On June 3, 2022, the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) finalized 
a package of regulatory changes to set biofuel volumes for 2020, 2021, and 2022 to reach the 
objectives of the RFS. The goals are to increase the amounts of renewable fuels to be blended 
into transportation fuel in increasing amounts each year. The renewable fuels in the RFS program 
must emit lower levels of GHGs relative to the petroleum fuel it replaces.  
CALIFORNIA CLIMATE CHANGE LEGISLATION 
California has established itself as a leader in addressing climate change through an evolving 
suite of legislation to set statewide GHG reduction targets and develop strategies to meet them. 
California was one of the first states to set legislative statewide GHG reduction targets in 2006, 
and again one of the first to set a long-term net zero target. The following provides a summary 
of the key legislative actions the State has taken to address climate change, with an overview 
presented in Figure 2-2. 
Executive Order (EO) S-3-05 was signed in 2005, which directed California to reduce statewide 
GHG emissions to 1990 levels by 2020 and to 80 percent below 1990 levels by 2050. A year later, 
the Global Warming Solutions Act of 2006 (Assembly Bill [AB] 32) was passed, establishing 
California’s current legislative GHG 
Reduction Targets include: 
• Reducing GHG emissions to 40% 
below 1990 levels by 2030 
• Reducing GHG emissions to 85% 
below 1990 levels by 2045, and 
achieving net zero emissions 
through carbon dioxide removal 


DRAFT
Oceanside General Plan Update 
2-6 
regulatory, reporting, and market mechanisms to achieve quantifiable reductions in GHG 
emissions. AB 32 put a cap on GHGs and set a target of reducing statewide emissions to 1990 
levels by 2020. In 2008, the California Air Resources Board (CARB) developed the Climate Change 
Scoping Plan: A Framework for Change (2008 Scoping Plan) as part of its role in implementing AB 
32 and EO S-3-05. The 2008 Scoping Plan, along with its update in 2014, described the approach 
California would take to reduce GHG emissions to achieve reduction targets and goals. In 2016, 
California achieved the AB 32 target of reducing GHG emissions to 1990 levels, 4 years ahead of 
schedule, based on its reported statewide 2016 inventory (CARB 2020). The annual statewide 
GHG inventories for 2017-2020 have shown that the state continues to achieve the AB 32 target. 
In September 2016, Senate Bill (SB) 32 codified into statute the midterm 2030 target of 40 percent 
below 1990 levels by 2030. In November 2017, CARB published California’s 2017 Climate Change 
Scoping Plan Update (2017 Scoping Plan) (CARB 2017), which laid out the framework for achieving 
the 2030 target established in SB 32. The 2017 Scoping Plan identifies GHG reductions by emissions 
sector to achieve a statewide emissions level that is 40 percent below 1990 levels by 2030.  
The California Climate Crisis Act (AB 1279), signed in September 2022, further established the 
State’s GHG emissions reduction targets of an 85 percent reduction in anthropogenic emissions 
below 1990 levels by 2045 and net zero GHG emissions by 2045.  
2022 CLIMATE CHANGE SCOPING PLAN 
CARB’s 2022 Scoping Plan for Achieving Carbon Neutrality (2022 Scoping Plan) assesses progress 
toward the statutory 2030 target, while laying out a path to achieving carbon neutrality no later 
than 2045. The 2022 Scoping Plan focuses on outcomes needed to achieve carbon neutrality by 
assessing paths for clean technology, energy deployment, natural and working lands, and others, 
and is designed to meet the State’s long-term climate objectives and support a range of 
economic, environmental, energy security, environmental justice, and public health priorities.  
While the 2022 Scoping Plan provides a framework for the State to meet its long-term GHG 
reduction targets, it also provides guidance for local jurisdictions on how their climate planning 
efforts can align with State priorities. The 2022 Scoping Plan provides an overview of the feasible 
and cost-effective pathways for climate mitigation, including key focus areas of electrifying most 
commercial and residential buildings, reducing vehicle with smart-growth infill development, 
preserving natural and working lands, achieving 100 percent renewable electricity, and achieving 
high rates of zero-emission vehicle adoption. Local jurisdictions are encouraged by CARB to align 
with these priorities in their climate planning efforts to support the State in meeting its 
aggressive GHG reduction targets.

Major Climate Legislation in the State of California 
2.4 Addressing Climate Change through Regional 
Collaboration  
Regional action and collaboration are needed to effectively address the climate emergency, and 
various programs have been developed in the San Diego region to help expand local capacity and 
synchronize efforts in climate change mitigation. The ability of individual jurisdictions to 
collaborate and coordinate efforts also presents opportunities for inter-jurisdictional 
coordination, leveraging economies of scale that can help reduce costs for program 
implementation, and positioning the region for state and federal funding opportunities. The 
following provides a selection of regional programs and initiatives in which the City engages to 
support regional and local climate change mitigation.  
SANDAG REGIONAL CLIMATE PLANNING FRAMEWORK 
The San Diego Association of Governments (SANDAG) is the region’s transportation planning 
agency which leads the Regional Climate Action Planning Framework (ReCAP). ReCAP identifies 
best practices and guidance for preparing CAPs for the San Diego region and assists in 
monitoring implementation of local CAPs. As part of the framework, jurisdictions are provided 
with biennial GHG emissions inventory updates and implementation tracking data. This effort 
provides essential support for jurisdictions to plan, monitor and update their adopted CAPs.  
SAN DIEGO COUNTY’S REGIONAL DECARBONIZATION FRAMEWORK 
San Diego County's science‐based Regional Decarbonization Framework promotes public and 
private interests working together to move the entire San Diego region toward zero carbon 
emissions in transportation, buildings, energy, and land use by mid‐century. The Regional 
Decarbonization Framework will help policymakers across San Diego identify ways their cities and 
the unincorporated areas of the county can decarbonize while working in harmony with neighboring 
jurisdictions as well as community, industry, environmental, business, and labor groups.

Oceanside General Plan Update 
2-8 
2.5 Climate Mitigation Efforts in Oceanside 
The City of Oceanside has a number of current plans and programs that enhance energy 
conservation, water efficiency, waste reduction, and active transportation. Implementing these 
programs and projects allows the City to protect and enhance natural resources, reduce the City's 
carbon footprint and save money for Oceanside ratepayers. Below is a short list of programs and 
related projects that the City is undertaking that support climate mitigation efforts. 
GREEN OCEANSIDE PROGRAM 
The Green Oceanside campaign was established to share resources and educate residents, 
businesses and visitors about how to engage in more sustainable everyday practices. Through 
collaboration and partnerships between City departments, local businesses, community groups, 
schools and residents, the Green Oceanside campaign provides an avenue for the public to learn 
how to actively protect the environment through community involvement and engagement by 
offering opportunities for people to participate in events and programs that provide a direct 
benefit to the local environment while reducing the City’s carbon footprint.  
ZERO WASTE PROGRAM 
The City’s Zero Waste Program provides resources to 
community members on ways to increase diversion of waste 
from landfills and information about compliance with the 
requirements of SB 1383. As part of the program, the City 
developed a citywide Zero Waste Plan, first adopted in 2012 
and updated in 2020, to outline a strategy to achieve a 90 
percent citywide waste diversion target.

2-9 
WATERSHED PROTECTION PROGRAM 
The goal of the Oceanside Watershed Protection Program is to improve water quality in local 
creeks, rivers, and the ocean, and comply with state and regional environmental regulations. This 
multi-faceted program is designed to achieve its goal by educating the community about 
pollution prevention practices. The Buena Vista 
Creek Restoration Planning Project is one of the 
many projects under this program, that will help 
improve existing riparian habitat, enhance water 
quality, and provide recreational and educational 
opportunities for the surrounding community. 
Efforts under this program to restore and enhance 
watersheds provides increased opportunity for 
carbon sequestration within Oceanside.  
WATER EFFICIENCY PROGRAM 
The City’s Water Conservation Master Plan, updated in 2020, was created to help the City meet 
its future water needs, and is the guiding document for Green Oceanside’s water conservation 
program. The City also maintains mandatory water use efficiency measures for its customers to 
ensure adequate water supplies even in drought conditions. Improving water efficiency reduces 
the energy required to supply and treat water, thereby reducing GHG emissions.  
PURE WATER OCEANSIDE PROGRAM 
Pure Water Oceanside aims to purify recycled 
water to create a new, local source of high-quality 
drinking water that is clean, safe, drought-proof 
and environmentally sound. The program will 
provide more than 30% of the City's water supply, 
allowing for reduced reliance on imported water 
which requires much more energy to deliver to 
Oceanside than local water supplies.

RENEWABLE ENERGY PROGRAM 
The goal of the program is to provide a secure 
energy source alternative that is below the cost 
spent for the same amount of energy purchased 
from San Diego Gas and Electric (SDG&E). 
Completed in 2013, the San Luis Rey Solar Panel 
Project, produces an alternative source of energy 
for the San Luis Rey Water Reclamation Facility 
that is renewable and resilient. 
The City has joined Clean Energy Alliance (CAE), 
a community choice aggregation program, which 
provides an alternative power source to SDG&E to 
provide a more cost-competitive choice of renewable energy. CEA began delivering electricity 
comprised of 50 percent renewable sources in April 2024.  
URBAN FORESTRY PROGRAM 
The Urban Forestry Program manages approximately 48,000 trees in the City’s rights-of-way, 
facilities, and parks. With over 200 different species, varieties, and cultivators, the City’s street 
tree population is diverse and provides significant benefits for residents and visitors. The Urban 
Forestry Program is responsible for a significant portion of the urban forests in the City. 
GREEN OCEANSIDE BUSINESS NETWORK 
Green Oceanside Business Network is a program by the City that seeks to spur innovation and 
environmental responsibility while helping Oceanside businesses prosper in the green economy. 
The program provides opportunities for local businesses to showcase and share practices that 
reduce waste and increase water and energy efficiency. 
GREEN OCEANSIDE KITCHEN 
This is a state-of-the-art food recovery and preservation 
facility designed to foster a sustainable food system and 
offer culinary learning opportunities dedicated to 
wasting nothing and feeding the community.

COMMERCIAL & MULTI-FAMILY FOOD SCRAPS RECYCLING PROGRAM 
The City and Waste Management launched the Commercial & Multi-Family Food Scraps 
Recycling Program in 2020 to recycle food waste and food-soiled paper in residents’ own bins. 
This program offers cost savings opportunities for business and puts the City one step closer to 
achieving a 75-90% diversion rate. 
BICYCLE MASTER PLAN  
The City of Oceanside 2008 Bicycle Master Plan 
combines practical solutions with community 
goals for developing and enhancing bicycle 
facilities in the City. The plan was updated in 
2017 to evaluate progress completed to date and 
consider additional improvements to bicycle 
infrastructure. The plan objectives included 
establishing facility types to be implemented 
and identifying points where the City’s bikeway 
system could integrate with the existing San 
Diego County regional bikeway system.  
PEDESTRIAN MASTER PLAN 
The City of Oceanside 2009 Pedestrian Master Plan identified opportunities for improvements to 
the walking environment throughout Oceanside to support community members in being able 
to safely walk to destinations. Improving the walking conditions in the City supports residents, 
workers, and visitors in opting to walk to destinations instead of driving.  
GO’SIDE SHUTTLE PROGRAM 
The City, in partnership with SANDAG and Visit Oceanside, operates low-fare fully electric shuttle 
rides in parts of Oceanside. The gO’side shuttle pilot project aims to provide connections to the 
Oceanside Transit Station and relieve traffic congestion and parking challenges in Downtown 
Oceanside. It also makes it easier for people to navigate the area without relying on a car.

The shuttle provides an accessible, zero-emissions option for people who want to access 
Oceanside’s shops and recreational activities, as well as dining, transit, healthcare, jobs and 
residential areas. The project is currently in its second year of operation.  
CITY FACILITIES ENERGY IMPROVEMENTS 
The City, in collaboration with ENGIE North America, is working on a transformative energy 
initiative aimed at enhancing sustainability, reducing costs and fostering community 
engagement. This initiative is projected to generate more than $26,000,000 in net savings for the 
City. It includes significant energy efficiency gains through a series of measures at City facilities, 
including integrating 1.6 megawatts of solar alongside a 250-kilowatt energy storage system, 
replacing and refurbishing HVAC units; implementing a battery energy storage system and an 
energy management system; installing new, efficient distribution transformers and generator 
heat pumps; and upgrading interior and exterior lighting to LEDs. 
2.6 Relationship to the General Plan Update 
In 2019, the City Council adopted Phase 1 of the General 
Plan Update and adopted two new General Plan 
elements addressing economic development and 
climate action. The Economic Development Element 
(EDE) informs future actions affecting the City’s fiscal 
resources and the local economy, centered around providing a high quality of life with economic 
prosperity, resilience, and collaboration. The Energy and Climate Action Element (ECAE) 
outlines goals and policies around the themes of energy efficiency/renewable energy, smart 
growth and multimodal transportation, zero waste, water conservation, local agriculture, urban 
greening, and sustainable consumption. These two new elements were accompanied by the City’s 
first CAP, which included both mandatory and voluntary emissions reduction measures to 
address citywide GHG emissions sources and set a pathway towards reaching GHG reduction 
targets aligned with state goals. The EDE, ECAE, and CAP constituted Phase 1 of the 
comprehensive General Plan Update and provided important guidance for the second phase of 
the General Plan Update, which involves updates to the City’s existing General Plan elements 
and developing specific and community plans to guide growth in the City. Components of Phase 
2 of the General Plan Update that are pertinent to the CAP Update are discussed below.  
The CAP Update is being completed 
as part of Phase 2 of the City’s 
comprehensive General Plan Update. 


DRAFT
Climate Action Plan Update 
2-13 
EFFICIENT AND COMPATIBLE LAND USE ELEMENT 
The City’s update to its Land Use Element, the Efficient and Compatible Land Use (ECLU) 
Element, determines how future growth will occur in Oceanside. The goals of the ECLU ensure 
compatibility between different land uses, accommodate demand for both housing and 
nonresidential uses, foster complete neighborhoods, and encourage efficient land use that 
creates synergies between different uses, enhances walkability and transit access, and reduces 
development pressure on outlying areas. The CAP Update has been developed to be consistent 
with the development patterns of the ECLU.  
SMART AND SUSTAINABLE CORRIDORS SPECIFIC PLAN  
The City’s Smart and Sustainable Corridors Specific Plan (SSCSP) outlines strategies for 
promoting infill and redevelopment along the Mission Avenue, Oceanside Boulevard, and Vista 
Way corridors in the City. The SSCSP regulatory document establishing land use and 
development standards for properties within the planning area. Prepared concurrently with the 
City’s updated General Plan, the SSCSP will implement General Plan goals and policies that 
promote efficient land use, a stronger sense of place, a range of mobility options, open space, 
and farmland preservation, social engagement, and enhanced visual quality. 
SOUTH MORRO HILLS COMMUNITY PLAN 
With a South Morro Hills Community Plan, the City seeks to preserve farmland by 
accommodating agritourism and housing while preserving agricultural resources and facilitating 
the long-term viability of farming operations. Consistent with the Agritourism Strategic Plan, 
the South Morro Hills Community Plan builds upon and refines the “agri-vision” developed by 
property owners in South Morro Hills. 
2.7 California Environmental Quality Act 
CEQA is a statute that requires public agencies to identify the significant environmental impacts 
of new development and other projects, including GHG emissions impacts, and to avoid or 
mitigate those impacts if it is feasible to do so. CEQA, in its implementing guidelines, includes 
provisions to streamline the environmental review process of projects that are consistent with a 
“plan for the reduction of greenhouse gas emissions” that meets specified criteria, which are 
outlined in CEQA Guidelines Section 15183.5(b)(1). The CAP Update has been developed to be 
used as a streamlining plan for GHG emissions.  


DRAFT
Oceanside General Plan Update 
2-14 
Under these provisions, a project that is consistent with the City’s “qualified” CAP is eligible for 
streamlined analysis of its GHG emissions impacts under CEQA. While project consistency with 
the CAP Update would ultimately need to be determined on a case-by-case basis, a consistent 
project would generally be one that is both consistent with the growth projections used to 
prepare the CAP Update and that incorporates all applicable measures and actions from the CAP 
Update. The incremental GHG emissions from projects found to be consistent with the CAP 
Update may be determined to not have a cumulative considerable impact under CEQA. Projects 
eligible to utilize these streamlining provisions will be able to tier from and/or incorporate by 
reference the CAP Update’s programmatic review of GHG emissions in their CEQA analysis of 
cumulative GHG emissions.

3 
Greenhouse Gas Emissions Inventory, 
Projections, and Targets 
3.1 Overview 
This chapter summarizes an accounting of greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions generated by 
activities within the City, which is used to inform the development of GHG reduction measures 
for the Climate Action Plan (CAP) Update. This accounting presents the City of Oceanside’s 
(City’s) baseline GHG emissions levels in 2018 and the anticipated increase in emissions if the 
City takes no local actions through the year 2045. Additionally, this chapter establishes targets 
for reducing GHG emissions consistent with statewide GHG reduction targets and goals for the 
years 2030, 2045, and 2050. The analyses presented here are updates from those included in the 
City’s 2019 CAP and provide an updated set of reference points for the development of GHG 
reduction measures to meet GHG reduction targets aligned with current state legislation.  
3.2 Greenhouse Gas Emissions Inventory  
GREENHOUSE GAS INVENTORY BACKGROUND 
The GHG emissions inventory provides the existing conditions for the CAP Update, identifying 
the contribution of different communitywide GHG emissions sources in Oceanside and 
establishing a baseline for forecasting future emissions. The inventory has informed the GHG 
reduction targets and is essential to effective monitoring of progress towards reaching 
established targets over time. The GHG emissions inventory presented here is for the calendar 
year 2018, which was provided by the San Diego Association of Governments (SANDAG) through 
the Regional Climate Action Planning Framework (ReCAP) Snapshot program (SANDAG 2023)1. 
An updated GHG emissions inventory is used to provide a baseline for forecasting future 
emissions from the most recently available data, and a consistent baseline for future emissions 
inventories that are expected to be provided by SANDAG on an ongoing basis. While previous 
 
1  The GHG emissions inventory provided through ReCAP is a “communitywide” emissions inventory, which covers all relevant GHG 
emissions sources and/or activities in the City of Oceanside boundary. Municipal government operations are considered within 
this communitywide inventory but are not explicitly disaggregated. 


DRAFT
Oceanside General Plan Update 
3-2 
GHG inventories have been completed for Oceanside, including a 2013 inventory for the City’s 
2019 CAP, the use of the updated baseline for 2018 allows for consistent comparison of emissions 
over time with the data sources and methods used by SANDAG. Consistent emissions tracking is 
essential to verify the effectiveness of the program and policy implementation for the CAP 
Update. The use of an updated emissions inventory baseline also informs development of 
updated GHG emissions forecasts and reduction targets consistent with anticipated growth in 
the City and state legislation. 
The 2018 GHG emissions inventory used in the CAP Update was provided to the City through the 
ReCAP program and has been modified to provide consistency with the in-progress General Plan 
Update land uses and encompass relevant emissions sources and/or activities in Oceanside. 
Vehicle miles traveled (VMT) data, which is used to calculate on-road transportation emissions, 
was updated from that used in the 2018 inventory as provided by the ReCAP Snapshot to utilize 
a land use and growth scenario consistent with the in-progress General Plan Update. The purpose 
of this exercise is to better reflect how GHG emissions may change in the future under 
implementation of the City’s General Plan. Additionally, emissions from the use of off-road 
equipment use have been added to the GHG inventory, which was not included in the 2018 ReCAP 
Snapshot inventory. These updates provide a more comprehensive assessment of emissions that 
allows for alignment with expected future growth.  
2018 GREENHOUSE GAS INVENTORY UPDATE 
The 2018 communitywide GHG emissions inventory demonstrates the contribution of different 
GHG emissions sources in Oceanside by emissions sector. The largest contributor to total 
emissions was fuel combustion in on-road vehicles, representing 51 percent of total emissions. 
The next two largest emissions sources were grid-purchased electricity and natural gas use in 
buildings and facilities within the City, contributing 22 percent and 18 percent to total emissions, 
respectively. The remaining emissions sectors, including solid waste, off-road transportation, 
water, and wastewater represent approximately nine (9) percent of total emissions. In total, 
communitywide emissions sources considered in the 2018 emissions inventory generated 
approximately 846,000 metric tons of carbon dioxide equivalent (MTCO2e). These results are 
presented in Figure 3-1 and Table 3-1, showing the total emissions by sector with the 
representative contribution to total communitywide emissions. More detail on the results and 
methods for the 2018 emissions inventory can be found in Appendix A.

3.3 Greenhouse Gas Emissions Forecasts 
The GHG emissions forecasts estimate how communitywide GHG emissions for Oceanside may 
grow given future increases in housing and economic growth, and how state and federal 
regulations may help to reduce local emissions. The results of the forecasts provide insight into 
changes in future emissions levels, and the necessary type and scale of actions that will be needed 
to align with state GHG reduction targets. The CAP Update includes two forecasts to represent 
future GHG conditions with and without the impact of local, state, and federal regulations as 
described below.  
The GHG emissions forecasts are presented as a sector-level assessment of near-term (2030) and 
long-term (2045 and 2050) GHG emissions projections based on current conditions under two 
scenarios. Under the first scenario, GHG emissions would grow from 2018 levels at the same rates 
as housing, population, employment, and vehicle travel, which is known as a business-as-usual 
(BAU) scenario forecast. The BAU forecast serves as a basis for understanding how emissions 
levels may change with growth, and how far GHG emissions will need to be reduced in future 
years to meet GHG reduction targets without considering future action to reduce GHG emissions. 
The second scenario considers the GHG reduction impact of local, state, and federal regulations, 
which is known as a legislative adjusted business-as-usual scenario (ABAU) forecast. The ABAU 
forecast provides a more detailed assessment of future GHG emissions by showing how currently 
adopted local, state and federal regulations will help reduce emissions in Oceanside. The future 
years selected for the GHG emissions forecasts are aligned with the GHG reduction target years 
under state legislation, 2030 for Senate Bill (SB) 32 and 2045 for Assembly Bill (AB) 1279, as well 
as 2050 to align with the in-progress General Plan Update horizon year. 
BUSINESS-AS-USUAL GHG EMISSIONS FORECAST  
The BAU forecast was developed by scaling communitywide GHG emissions from the 2018 
emissions inventory based on the City’s growth in housing, jobs, and vehicle travel. More detail 
on the approach for forecasting specific GHG emissions sectors is provided in Appendix A. VMT 
for on-road transportation is also essential for understanding how future GHG emissions may 
change in Oceanside. VMT was modeled using the SANDAG ABM2+ model version 14.3.0, with 
adjustments made to account for the growth in housing and overall development consistent with 
the land uses included in the City’s in-progress General Plan Update.  
The results of the BAU forecast show that communitywide GHG emissions would be expected to 
grow, given no further GHG reduction efforts beyond 2018, as shown in Table3-2. While a more 
likely scenario for future GHG emissions can be seen in the ABAU forecast, the BAU scenario 
provides the basis for understanding the GHG impact of anticipated growth in the City.

LEGISLATIVE ADJUSTED BUSINESS-AS-USUAL GHG EMISSIONS FORECAST  
The ABAU forecast was developed by accounting for the expected GHG reductions in 
communitywide GHG emissions in Oceanside as a result of adopted local, state, and federal 
regulations. The GHG reductions considered in the ABAU forecast fall into three categories: 
building efficiency standards, fuel efficiency standards and electric vehicle sale requirements, 
and renewable electricity requirements. The full list of legislation considered in the ABAU 
forecast is provided in Appendix A. In addition, since the development of the 2019 CAP, the City 
of Oceanside has committed to joining the Clean Energy Alliance (CEA). Under CEA membership, 
electricity accounts in Oceanside will be automatically enrolled at the 50 percent renewable 
electricity option starting in 2024. The ABAU scenario considers the GHG reductions that are 
expected to be realized through the City joining CEA. 
The results of the ABAU forecast show that emissions are expected to decline from 2018 levels 
through 2050, as shown in Table 3-3.

3.4 Greenhouse Gas Reduction Targets 
A primary purpose of the CAP Update is to align the City of Oceanside’s GHG reduction targets 
with the most recent state legislation. Since the 2019 CAP was adopted, AB 1279 was signed into 
law, which requires the State to develop and implement a strategy for achieving statewide net-
zero emissions by 2045 or sooner, which includes an 85 percent reduction in anthropogenic GHG 
emissions by 2045 with remaining emissions offset by carbon dioxide removal. The state’s 
strategy for meeting the AB 1279 target, as outlined in the 2022 Climate Change Scoping Plan, 
indicates that net-zero emissions would be achieved through CO2 removal through both carbon 
sequestration in natural lands and mechanical carbon capture and storage technologies. Carbon 
sequestration and CO2 removal and carbon capture and storage technologies are being evaluated 
by the state, and it is not feasible for local jurisdictions, including Oceanside, to scale these 
solutions independently to achieve net-zero GHG emissions by 2045.

Oceanside General Plan Update 
3-8 
Therefore, to align with recent state legislation, the CAP Update includes a long-term GHG 
reduction target in alignment with AB 1279. The 2030 GHG reduction target of the CAP update 
is maintaining consistency with the 2019 CAP by aligning with SB 32. Additionally, a 2050 target 
is being added to align with the in-progress General Plan Update horizon year.  
ALIGNING WITH STATEWIDE TARGETS 
The primary statewide GHG reduction targets relevant to the CAP Update are those established 
by SB 32 of 2016 and AB 1279 of 2022, which include: 
• 
Reducing statewide GHG emissions 40 percent below 1990 levels by 2030 (SB 32), and 
• 
Reducing statewide anthropogenic GHG emissions 85 percent below 1990 levels before 
2045 (AB 1279). 
With the State’s adoption of these legislative targets, the California Air Resources Board (CARB) 
was directed to develop and implement strategies to reduce statewide emissions consistent with 
the targets through a Climate Change Scoping Plan (Scoping Plan). The Scoping Plan outlines 
the State’s strategy to reduce GHG emissions in each economic sector and is regularly updated 
to reflect the most recent trends, technology, and legislative reduction targets. California’s 2017 
Climate Change Scoping Plan was developed and adopted to outline the State’s strategy for 
reducing GHG emissions to meet the SB 32 legislative reduction target. The most recent Scoping 
Plan, The 2022 Scoping Plan for Achieving Carbon Neutrality, was similarly developed for meeting 
the AB 1279 GHG reduction target. The pathways for statewide GHG emissions reductions in both 
versions of the Scoping Plan provide a framework for developing local GHG reduction targets.  
GREENHOUSE GAS REDUCTION TARGETS FOR OCEANSIDE 
The Scoping Plan identifies local governments as essential partners in achieving California’s GHG 
reduction goals. One way for local governments to support the state goals is to establish GHG 
reduction targets that align with state targets and the strategies outlined in the Scoping Plan. The 
City of Oceanside has aligned its local GHG reduction targets in the CAP Update with the Scoping 
Plan’s future GHG emissions scenarios for economic sectors that are relevant to local conditions. 
This allows the City to set GHG reduction targets that are directly aligned with the State’s 
strategies for reducing GHG emissions and are based on a more recent GHG inventory year.  


DRAFT
Climate Action Plan Update 
3-9 
Since the State compares its GHG reductions targets against a 1990 baseline, and the City of 
Oceanside does not have a 1990 GHG inventory available, local targets for the must be calculated 
based on the State’s historical GHG emissions levels and the future pathways of the Scoping Plans. 
More detail on how Oceanside’s GHG reduction targets were calculated, and alignment with the 
Scoping Plan GHG emissions sectors and modeling, is available in Appendix A. 
The GHG reduction targets for the CAP Update demonstrate consistency with state legislation 
through locally specific GHG target levels based on the 2018 GHG emissions inventory. The GHG 
reduction targets are aligned with the key statewide reduction targets and target years of SB 32 and 
AB 1279, as well as an additional long-term target for the year 2050 to align with the in-progress 
General Plan Update horizon year. The GHG reduction targets for the CAP Update include: 
• 
Reducing emissions 27 percent below 2018 levels by 2030 (aligned with SB 32),  
• 
Reducing emissions 90 percent below 2018 levels by 2045 (aligned with AB 1279), and 
• 
Maintaining emissions 90 percent below 2018 levels through 2050 (General Plan Update 
horizon year). 
The City of Oceanside’s updated GHG reduction targets for the years 2030, 2045, and 2050 are 
provided in Table 3-5.

3.5 Reaching the Targets 
The purpose of the CAP Update is to develop strategies the City can implement to reduce 
communitywide GHG emissions consistent with local reduction targets. With the GHG emissions 
forecasts and reduction targets presented in this chapter, the City can assess the GHG reductions it 
will need to achieve to meet the above-noted reduction targets by bridging the “emissions gap.” The 
emissions gap demonstrates the future expected GHG emissions levels from the ABAU forecast for 
each GHG emissions sector, and additional GHG reductions that will need to be achieved by the GHG 
reduction measures of the CAP Update to reduce emissions in alignment with state GHG reduction 
targets. Local actions in the CAP Update are necessary for the City to achieve its targets. Table 3-6 
and Figure 3-3 provide a summary of the ABAU forecast in comparison with the updated GHG 
reduction targets, and the emissions gap that would need to be reduced to meet the targets.

4-1 
4 
Greenhouse Gas Reduction Measures 
and Actions 
4.1 Overview 
The City of Oceanside (City) seeks to assist its residents, workers, and visitors in rendering their 
everyday activities more sustainable and less carbon-intensive. With this vision, the City has 
developed a suite of greenhouse gas (GHG) reduction measures and actions that will reduce the 
carbon-intensity of municipal operations, expand existing City programs to support community 
members in their efforts to reduce their carbon footprint, and establish new building codes that 
reduce energy use and expand access to renewable energy in new and existing development. All 
of these measures and actions are designed to facilitate the decarbonization of the local 
economy. Together, these measures and actions strike a balance between the need to meet long-
term GHG reduction targets and the current availability of resources necessary to effectively 
implement measures. This chapter presents the GHG reduction measures and actions that are 
the core of the Climate Action Plan Update (CAP Update).  
The following sections outline the GHG reduction framework developed for this CAP Update and 
summarize the factors that shaped the framework. Further on, the chapter presents a summary 
of each GHG reduction measure and the associated actions for implementation.  
4.2 Greenhouse Gas Reduction Framework 
The GHG reduction measures identified in this CAP Update are organized under six focus sectors. 
These sectors generally align with the 2018 GHG emissions inventory sources, but they are not 
exactly the same as the emission inventory sectors. This is because some measures are cross-
cutting in nature, while others are intended to provide additional community benefits while 
reducing emissions from sources not accounted for in the City’s GHG emissions inventory. The 
six focus sectors under which the GHG reduction measures are organized are listed in Figure 4-1 
along with a brief description of the type of measures included in the focus sector. 
 
 


DRAFT
Oceanside General Plan Update 
4-2 
 
 
 
 
 
ENERGY 
Includes measures that reduce emissions in the 
natural gas and electricity GHG inventory sectors, 
which primarily encompasses energy used in 
buildings. 
 
TRANSPORTATION 
Includes measures that reduce emissions in the on-
road transportation and off-road transportation GHG 
inventory sectors, focusing on reducing vehicle travel 
and shifting vehicles and equipment to electricity 
and renewable fuels. 
 
SOLID WASTE 
Includes measures that reduce emissions resulting 
from the decay of organic materials in landfills, 
including increasing waste diversion and composting. 
 
WATER 
Includes measures that reduce water consumption 
and the electricity emissions associated with the 
City’s water supply. 
 
CARBON 
SEQUESTRATION 
Reduces the City’s overall GHG emissions 
contribution by protecting natural lands and 
expanding the urban forests. 
 
LOCAL GREEN 
ECONOMY 
Intended to reduce emissions from consumer products 
and support local job growth in emerging sectors.  
Figure 4-1 
Greenhouse Gas Reduction Measures Focus Sectors 
Measures in each sector would be implemented through discrete implementation actions. 
Implementation actions are labeled as either “key” or “supportive” to highlight how the actions 
under each measure interact. Key actions are core to the implementation of the GHG reduction 
measure as a whole and are tied directly to the GHG reductions that are anticipated to be achieved 
from measures. Supportive actions are more focused towards supporting the City and community 
members in implementing and tracking progress of each measure. Both action types work 
together to provide transparency in how GHG reduction measures can be successfully 


DRAFT
Climate Action Plan Update 
4-3 
implemented. Additionally, some actions have been labeled as “inclusive,” which highlights 
where equity considerations have been integrated to ensure that the economic, environmental, 
and social benefits of measures extend to more community members and that potential negative 
impacts on low-income or historically disadvantaged communities are minimized. 
The measures and implementing actions discussed in this chapter were developed based on a 
combination of factors, including the results of the City’s GHG emissions inventories and 
forecasts, feedback from the public and stakeholders, and the best climate action planning 
guidance available to-date. Figure 4-2 describes the hierarchy and provides definitions for 
sectors, measures, and actions developed for the CAP. 
 
Figure 4-2 
Structure of Greenhouse Gas Reduction Measures and Implementing Actions 
4.3 GHG Reduction Summary 
In preparation of the CAP Update, a GHG quantification analysis was conducted to estimate 
potential emissions reductions associated with all measures. Implementation of the measures is 
estimated to result in 55,301 metric tons of carbon dioxide equivalent (MTCO2e) of GHG 
reductions in 2030; 222,773 MTCO2e in 2045; and 228,734 MTCO2e in 2050. The quantification 
analysis demonstrated that the total estimated reductions from all GHG reduction measures 
would be sufficient to meet the 2030 target. The results of the quantification analysis are shown 
in Table 4-1 below.

While the GHG reduction targets for 2045 and 2050 are not met with quantified measures alone, 
the measures presented here are intended to be feasibly implementable with current City 
resources and cost-effective for community members. The measures have been developed to set 
the City on a pathway that aligns with the statewide GHG reduction targets and demonstrate 
alignment with the State’s key priorities of building electrification, preserving natural lands, 
infill-focused development, and expanding zero-emission vehicle access. The quantified GHG 
reductions for measures have been developed to ensure that near-term implementation actions 
can be substantially supported. However, some measures include longer-term actions that are 
supportive of the State’s priorities but have not been quantified due to a lack of data or the 
inability to define programs in sufficient detail at this time. The City will monitor progress 
towards meeting near- and long-term reduction targets and update the CAP regularly to evaluate 
how new technologies, funding opportunities, and state and federal legislation may support the 
City in reaching long-term CAP goals. The City will also explore additional measures and 
expansion of programs as GHG reduction trends are monitored over time. More detail on the 
City’s monitoring procedures and potential expansion of measures is provided in Chapter 5 
Implementation and Monitoring. 
4.4 Detailed GHG Reduction Measures and 
Implementing Actions 
The 
following 
sections 
provide

ENERGY 
GHG emissions from energy consumption and generation represented 39% of the City’s total 
emissions in 2018. Even as electricity-related emissions are reduced in the future through 
procurement of renewable electricity from joining Clean Energy Alliance (CEA), natural gas 
consumption will continue to represent a significant proportion of the City’s total GHG 
emissions through 2045 and 2050. The GHG reduction measures and actions included in the 
Energy sector strive to promote energy efficiency, increase renewable energy availability, and 
reduce future consumption of natural gas. 
 
 
Measure E-1 
Increase renewable energy availability by providing 100% renewable 
electricity through the Clean Energy Alliance (CEA) by 2030 with a 95% 
participation rate.  
Year 
2030 
2045 
2050 
GHG Reduction 
Potential (MTCO2e) 
7,182 
0 
0 
To reduce emissions associated with electricity, the City plans to increase the availability of 
renewable energy to community members. The City has already made significant progress by 
joining the CEA, a community choice aggregation (CCA) program, providing residents and 
businesses with 50% renewable electricity as a default for those who do not opt-out as of April 
2024. The City can further the impact of joining CEA by purchasing 100% renewable electricity 
and increasing the default electricity rate to 100% renewable electricity for accounts in the 
community. The City will also enroll its own accounts in the 100% renewable/zero-carbon 
option, informed by cost estimates of such a transition. Regular outreach campaigns will be 
conducted to encourage continued participation in CEA, which includes sharing information 
online and promoting CEA participation at community events.

Oceanside General Plan Update
