Measure E-1 Implementing Actions 
Action E-1.1 
Provide renewable energy as a default to all electricity accounts in the 
community through CEA at 50% renewables mix rate by 2025 at the 
time of enrollment. 
 
Action E-1.2 
Enroll 100% of City accounts in 100% renewable/zero-carbon option 
from CEA by 2030. 
 
Action E-1.3 
Update the agreement with CEA to increase the default for all CEA 
customers in the community to 100% renewable energy by 2030.  
 
Action E-1.4 
Annually track CEA participation rates, including opt-out and opt-
down trends, and work with CEA to develop strategies for maintaining 
or increasing participation.  
 
Action E-1.5 
Conduct annual community engagement campaigns to promote CEA 
renewable energy choices and programs through print, social media 
and digital media through Green Oceanside sustainability campaigns 
and community events.  
 
 
 
Measure E-2 
Increase behind-the-meter solar photovoltaic (PV) capacity in the City 
to 125 megawatts (MW) by 2030 and 165 MW by 2045.  
Year

To enhance the resilience of the City's energy infrastructure and 
reduce reliance on grid supplied electricity, the City plans to 
increase the availability of local renewable energy in Oceanside. 
Through incremental expansion of the PV capacity at both City- 
and privately-owned properties, the City plans to achieve at least 
125 MW of communitywide solar capacity by 2030 and 165 MW 
by 2045. The City will ensure the expansion of solar infrastructure 
through the adoption of building code standards that require 
solar on all new construction and the installation of additional 
solar arrays at City-owned facilities.

The City will also support the voluntary installation of solar 
infrastructure through an expedited permitting process, 
promotion of financing options, and collaboration with CEA to 
support installation of solar at low-income households. 
Measure E-2 Implementing Actions 
Action E-2.1 
By 2026, adopt a building code standard that requires solar PV 
systems on all new residential and non-residential construction and 
qualifying major renovations. This may be accomplished either 
through the 2025 Building Energy Efficiency Standards or a reach code 
designed to exceed the 2025 Building Energy Efficiency Standards so 
that all new construction has solar PV incorporated into the design.  
 
Action E-2.2 
Implement the recommendations included in the 2023 “Energy 
Conservation Measures Analysis” completed for municipal facilities 
to install solar PV systems and battery storage at City facilities by 
including projects in the City's Capital Improvement Plan, as funding 
becomes available.  
 
Action E-2.3 
Partner with CEA to explore a program to utilize CEA residual revenue 
to install rooftop solar at low-income households at little to no cost 
to homeowners.  
 
Action E-2.4 
Promote available solar rebates and financing options through Green 
Oceanside and by providing a venue for CEA, San Diego Gas and 
Electric (SDG&E) and other local entities to hold workshops on 
rebates and financing options. Some solar rebates available in 
California are California Solar Initiative (CSI), Self-Generation 
Incentive Program (SGIP), the SGIP Equity Resiliency Rebate 
Program, and the Federal Solar Tax Credit Program. Solar incentive 
programs offered by SDG&E are Solar Incentive Program (SIP), 
Virtual Net Metering (VNM), and Power Saver Rewards Program.  
 
Action E-2.5 
Maintain expedited permitting for residential solar PV systems.

Measure E-3 
Launch an “Energy Upgrade Oceanside Program” with a goal of 
achieving 400 building energy efficiency retrofits by 2030 and 60,000 
by 2045 with primary end uses converted to electric.  
Year 
2030 
2045 
2050 
GHG Reduction 
Potential (MTCO2e) 
855 
169,069 
169,069 
To reduce emissions from energy use in existing buildings and support more efficient homes 
and businesses, the City will develop and launch an “Energy Upgrade Oceanside Program” to 
strategically transition Oceanside towards more efficient and primarily electricity-powered 
buildings. Initial steps would include adoption of a “reach code” to require retrofits and 
renovations of specified scope to implement cost-effective efficiency upgrades to meet an 
efficiency level based on a scoring criterion. The implementation of these upgrades will be 
supported through additional funding opportunities and sharing information on cost-saving 
opportunities such as tax credits and rebates. Longer-term decarbonization of buildings will 
require broader strategies and mechanisms to support electrification of existing buildings that 
does not increase costs to residents and businesses. To this end, the City will develop a detailed 
existing building decarbonization strategy that analyzes the specific challenges and 
opportunities for electrification in Oceanside and develops a phased long-term strategy for 
retrofitting the majority of buildings in the city. To ensure equitable implementation of 
measures and avoid unintended consequences, the City will track data related to energy 
upgrades and associated tenant displacement or 
disproportionate rent increases. If detrimental 
impacts are found to occur, the City will assess the 
feasibility of developing anti-displacement actions. 
As part of the Energy Upgrade Oceanside Program, 
the City will also retrofit its own buildings and 
facilities to improve efficiency and switch to electric 
end-uses where feasible and cost effective.

Measure E-3 Implementing Actions 
Action E-3.1 
Develop an “Existing Building Energy Conservation Ordinance” 
requiring existing buildings with qualifying improvements (addition 
of 750 square feet [sf] for residential, 1,500 sf or 15% above existing 
sf for non-residential) to concurrently implement energy efficient 
upgrades. Provide options to select and implement efficiency 
measures from a prescriptive list, which collectively achieve an 
efficiency level based on a scoring criterion. The analysis should 
identify which efficiency measures are considered cost effective for 
different building use types.  
 
Action E-3.2 
Adopt the “Existing Building Conservation Ordinance” consistent 
with the results of a cost-effectiveness analysis in Action E-3.1 by 
2026.  
 
Action E-3.3 
Develop a tracking system for the types of energy efficiency upgrades 
completed by applicants from the “Existing Building Conservation 
Ordinance” within the existing permit database software, including 
building primary use, annual energy consumption, square footage of 
retrofits, types of upgrades completed, and end uses electrified.  
 
Action E-3.4 
Every three years, following adoption of the “Existing Building 
Conservation Ordinance,” review the ordinance’s effectiveness in 
encouraging residents to perform cost-positive energy conservation 
measures and increase the stringency of the program or adjust the 
threshold of qualifying improvements as needed to reduce 
communitywide energy consumption.

efficiency upgrades, such as SDG&E rebates and incentives, SDG&E

Measure E-4 
Require that all new construction be electric ready by 2026.  
Year 
2030 
2045 
2050 
GHG Reduction 
Potential (MTCO2e) 
930  
6,464  
8,166  
To reduce dependence on fossil fuels in the future, the City plans to adopt and enforce the 
upcoming 2025 Building Energy Efficiency Standards. For single family residential buildings, the 
anticipated state standards include heat pump space heaters as well as heat pump water heaters 
or solar water heating system as prescriptive requirements in all climate zones. The proposed 
standards set heat pump water heaters as the prescriptive baseline for individual systems serving 
individual dwelling units in multifamily buildings with 3 or fewer habitable stories. The proposed 
standards also include mandatory requirements for electric ready multifamily buildings. 
Specifically, the standards include requirements to ensure that the entire building’s electrical 
systems have sufficient capacity to supply full rated amperage for all electric ready appliances, 
including heat pump space heaters, electric cooktops and electric clothes dryers in accordance 
with the California Electrical Code. If the ultimately adopted standards deviate from the 
requirements outlined here, the City will need to consider adoption of reach codes that achieve 
comparable reductions.  
These requirements applied to new construction will reduce building energy-related emissions 
and prepare the city for future electrification efforts. Through this measure, the City intends to 
make the transition to electric power easier for the community and ensure long-term cost savings 
for residents and businesses. The City plans to respond to the increased demand for contractors, 
technicians, and construction workers with expertise in electrical systems by partnering with 
regional organizations and providing training to the local workforce. Additionally, all newly 
constructed City facilities will be all electric, starting in 2030.

electric readiness in new buildings. If the 2025 Building Energy 
Efficiency Standards deviate from the proposals outlined above, the 
City will evaluate a reach code designed to meet the standards as 
proposed.

through the permitting counter, the City’s website, and the Green

exemption for end uses that do not have feasible electric alternatives 
at the time of design.

Climate Action Plan Update 
4-15 
 
TRANSPORTATION 
Transportation was the largest GHG emissions source in the City in 2018, representing 51% of 
total emissions, and will continue to be the largest GHG emissions source through 2045 and 2050. 
The key lever the City has to reduce transportation-related emissions is through a reduction in 
vehicle miles traveled (VMT) and facilitating the increased adoption of zero emission vehicles 
and equipment, which are the focus of the measures and actions in the Transportation sector. 
 
Measure TR-1 
Reduce per-capita vehicle miles traveled (VMT) 3% by 2030 and 6% by 
2045 from 2016 levels through transportation demand management 
(TDM) strategies.  
Year 
2030 
2045 
2050 
GHG Reduction 
Potential (MTCO2e) 
Not Quantified 
Not Quantified 
Not Quantified 
To reduce emissions associated with vehicular travel, the City plans to require and support TDM 
strategies to reduce VMT through multiple actions. A primary action is to lower the compliance 
threshold for the City’s current TDM Ordinance to 40 daily commute trips. This will serve to capture 
additional employers and increase the effectiveness of the ordinance. The City will support 
employers in complying with these new requirements by providing model TDM plans and providing 
information about cost effective TDM measures, such as utilizing the San Diego Association of 
Governments iCommute program. The City will also continue to promote, implement, and expand 
micro-transit options and support City employee commute trip reductions. The North County 
Transit District (NCTD) plans to launch a pilot micro-transit program called NCTD+ in north county 
cities. NCTD+ is an on-demand transit service that provides shared rides within a service zone. 
NCTD+ complements existing public transit and offers first and last mile connections to rail stations 
and transit centers. The first service zone is planned to launch in June 2024 in San Marcos, with 
potential expansion to other north county cities if the program is successful.  
 
 


DRAFT
Oceanside General Plan Update

Measure TR-1 Implementing Actions 
Action TR-1.1 
By 2026, amend the Transportation Demand Management Ordinance 
(Zoning Ordinance 3050) to require businesses that generate 40 or more 
daily commute trips to comply with the ordinance. Zoning Ordinance 
3050 will also be amended to be applicable to businesses that generate 
40 or more daily commute trips in redevelopment projects and at sites 
where a conditional use permit is being issued/updated.  
 
Action TR-1.2 
Develop a model TDM plan and provide education on compliance and 
reporting and resources for implementing TDM measures for 
businesses subject to Zoning Ordinance 3050.  
 
Action TR-1.3 
Support proposed GP policies IM 4-103 and 4-104 by implementing 
an outreach and educational campaign targeted to businesses to 
promote TDM strategies and encourage participation in the San 
Diego Association of Governments’ (SANDAG’s) iCommute program. 
 
Action TR-1.4 
Reduce City employee VMT by providing subsidized transit passes, a 
carpool connection program, preferential parking for carpools, and 
setting pricing for employee parking to provide parking cash out 
opportunities.  
 
Action TR-1.5 
Support proposed GP policy IM 4-89 by partnering with SANDAG and 
North County Transit District (NCTD) to expand the micro-transit 
electric vehicle (EV) shuttle program to operate in additional areas of 
the city, including the Smart and Sustainable Corridors Specific Plan 
planning area.  
 
Action TR-1.6 
Support proposed GP policies ECLU 3-30 and 3-32 by requiring that 
new mixed-use developments promote linkages with adjacent uses 
and provide adequate and secure bike parking facilities, as well as 
allowing for mobility and telework amenities as a part of public 
benefits packages.

Through this measure, the City aims to increase ZEV adoption by improving the EV charging 
infrastructure in Oceanside, which would reduce emissions generated by driving gasoline- and 
diesel-fueled vehicles. The City plans to adopt ordinances to require EV charging facilities at new 
and modified gas stations and commercial centers, as well as auxiliary power at delivery docks 
for reduced truck idling. The City will also develop 
a strategy to transition the municipal fleet to 
ZEVs by 2045. To support the community in the 
transition to ZEVs the City will promote 
incentives offered by various agencies. Measure 
TR-3 is intended to support the implementation 
of the State’s ZEV sale requirements of the 
Advanced Clean Cars (ACC) II and Advanced 
Clean Fleets (ACF) rules.

renewable fuels in the City’s bid procurement process by 2026, 
providing preference to contractors that utilize electric powered 
and/or renewable diesel fueled equipment.

Incentivize infill development through implementation of smart

The City’s proposed General Plan Update and other existing adopted City documents include 
policies that focus on smart growth principles and are intended to support and incentivize growth 
and mobility in priority infill areas, thereby providing an essential component of the City's strategy 
for reducing per-capita VMT and meeting local GHG reduction targets. This concerted effort across 
multiple general plan elements ensure residents and workers in Oceanside can perform daily 
activities more efficiently, and with less reliance on vehicle travel. The General Plan elements that 
include policies contributing to this vision for Oceanside include: 
• 
Efficient and Compatible Land Use (ECLU) Element, 
• 
Integrated Mobility (IM) Element, and 
• 
Smart and Sustainable Corridors Specific Plan (SSCSP). 
These proposed General Plan Update elements are in addition to the pending Coast Highway 
Vision and Strategic Plan, which promotes smart growth principles. They also include the Coast 
Highway Corridor to perform complete streets upgrades to the Coast Highway Corridor Design 
complete streets projects, and the Coast Highway Incentive District (CHID). The overall goal is 
to concentrate new development west of Interstate 5 along the Coast Highway Corridor and 
create a more walkable and accessible environment for residents, workers and visitors.  
The combination of these General Plan elements and plans provides the City with a growth 
framework that prioritizes infill and neighborhood scale developments with complete streets 
principles that enable access to existing transit and essential services through walking, biking, 
and micro mobility options. The City intends to incentivize development in these priority areas 
by streamlining the development review process for projects that align with form-based 
standards and include community benefit features.

WATER 
The Water sector represents a small proportion of the City’s total emissions, only 1% in 2018; 
however, the City has significant control over this emissions source through the ability to utilize 
local water supplies, reduction in demand, and low-carbon electricity sources for pumping, 
treating, and delivering water to customers.  
 
Measure W-1 
Reduce per-capita water consumption in the City by 6% by 2030, 
continued through 2045, compared to 2018 levels.  
Year 
2030 
2045 
2050 
GHG Reduction 
Potential (MTCO2e) 
187 
0 
0 
Through this measure, the City aims to reduce per-capita water consumption and in turn reduce 
the upstream electricity emissions associated with the water sector by implementing a Water 
Conservation Plan, City Facilities Water Efficiency Plan, and adopting a Model Water Efficient 
Landscape Ordinance (MWELO). Through actions like water-efficient building practices, low-
water landscaping, plumbing code updates, and outreach efforts, the City plans a phased 
reduction in per-capita water consumption by 2045. Additionally, the City plans on adopting 
ordinances to require water-efficient fixtures in new 
renovated buildings, distributing water conservation kits 
to low-income families, promoting water disclosure 
programs for businesses, and installing low-water use 
landscaping in City-owned buildings. To support the 
community, the City plans to continue offering rebates 
on water-efficient fixtures and use Green Oceanside for 
maintaining outreach efforts.

mechanisms for compliance should be clearly defined and provided 
to the community for input prior to ordinance adoption.

residential buildings by 50% through the Green Oceanside Business 
Network and direct engagement with businesses that are large water 
users.  
 
Action W-1.9

require drought-tolerant, water-conserving, and/or native landscaping 
in new development and redevelopment projects.

Enhancing and protecting the carbon sequestration potential of natural lands and the urban 
forest in Oceanside are key to supporting the State’s “30x30” initiative to protect 30% of the 
state’s natural lands and coastal waters by 2030. Relying on natural solutions to mitigate climate 
change can also enhance the City’s ability to adapt to potential climate change impacts.

LOCAL GREEN ECONOMY 
Encouraging residents and visitors to shop locally and supporting local job growth can reduce 
local and global GHG emissions, as well as support the initiatives of the Economic Development 
Element. Due to the complex interaction between local consumer choices and global supply 
chain emissions the measure and actions presented here are not quantified for local GHG 
reductions in the CAP Update.

residents and promoting local businesses.  
Year

With this measure, the City aims to promote sustainable local growth and equity through a 
multifaceted approach. The City plans to take initiatives including partnering with regional 
educational institutions to develop a local workforce equipped with new skills, documenting and 
promoting green practices in local businesses, assisting businesses to apply for sustainability 
grants, and educating the community on environmentally conscious consumption. The City also 
intends to promote local green businesses by educating Oceanside residents and visitors on the 
environmental benefits of buying locally produced goods. The City plans to use its own social 
media accounts and campaigns, including Visit Oceanside and Green Oceanside. Overall, with 
the focus on developing a skilled local workforce for a low carbon economy and collaboration 
with educational institutions, the City intends to enhance local workforce skills, promote local 
businesses, and reduce GHG emissions associated with consumer products.

Climate Action Plan Update 
5-1 
5 
Implementation and Monitoring 
This chapter outlines the City of Oceanside’s (City’s) approach for implementing the Climate 
Action Plan (CAP) Update and the greenhouse gas (GHG) reduction measures included herein. 
Successful implementation of the CAP Update will require a coordinated effort between City 
departments, stakeholders and the community as a whole. This chapter includes an 
Implementation and Monitoring Matrix where the responsible department(s) and timelines for 
implementation, as well as the key-performance indicators (KPIs) for each GHG reduction 
measure are clearly identified. KPIs are essential for the City to understand whether the programs 
and policies include in the GHG reduction measures are being effectively implemented and 
reaching the level of success needed to reduce communitywide GHG emissions in line with 
adopted GHG reduction targets. The City’s strategy for monitoring and reporting the success of 
measures is also outlined in this chapter, with the timelines for reporting progress and when 
updates to measure, and the CAP as a whole, would be initiated. Furthermore, the applicability 
of California Environmental Quality Act (CEQA) streamlining to the CAP Update is also discussed 
in this chapter. 
5.1 Implementation Strategy 
Successful implementation of the CAP Update requires that GHG reduction measures and the 
associated implementation actions are defined with specificity to allow City departments and 
staff to understand their role in implementation. Each GHG reduction measure includes a suite 
of actions that are divided amongst City departments, with a timeline for implementation 
provided for each. Additionally, GHG reduction measures that have a quantified GHG reduction 
potential have KPIs that are tied to GHG emissions reducing activities. More information about 
the implementation of individual GHG reduction measures and actions is provided at the end of 
this chapter, including KPIs for GHG reduction measures in Table 5-1 and an Implementation and 
Monitoring Matrix in Table 5-2.  
While individual departments are assigned certain implementation actions, interdepartmental 
collaboration will be essential to meeting the CAP Update goals. As such, City staff will work to 
develop internal procedures for regular coordination on CAP implementation. The initiatives 
that will be undertaken to coordinate implementation include: 


DRAFT
Oceanside General Plan Update 
5-2 
• 
Hiring or designation of a Climate Action Plan Administrator in Fiscal Year 2024-2025 
that will serve as an interdepartmental coordinator for CAP implementation and 
collection of data for monitoring CAP implementation progress. 
• 
Hosting quarterly meetings with City department leadership to discuss CAP 
implementation status, and understand challenges and needs of departments to support 
implementation of future actions.  
• 
Performing annual presentations to City Council and relevant commissions on CAP 
implementation status and implementation priorities for the upcoming year.  
REGIONAL COLLABORATION 
Regional collaboration is essential for successful CAP implementation. The City has engaged in 
various regional efforts, including the San Diego Regional Climate Collaborative and development 
of the San Diego County Regional Decarbonization Framework, and will continue to participate in 
and support other regional efforts for climate mitigation and adaptation. Regional engagement 
provides many benefits for the City’s CAP implementation efforts, including: 
• 
Helping to build staff capacity with 
regional programs and resources, 
• 
Informing local decision-making by 
understanding regional best practices 
and funding opportunities, 
• 
Access to economies of scale that reduce 
costs for program implementation for 
individual jurisdictions,  
• 
Enhancing the region’s eligibility for 
federal and state funding, and 
• 
Inspiring inter-jurisdictional 
coordination on other matters of mutual 
interest and concern. 
Some specific regional collaboration opportunities are highlighted in the implementation 
actions for GHG reduction measures; however, more opportunities are expected to materialize 
as the region pursues federal and state funding for programs that can support CAP 
implementation for individual jurisdictions. 
gO’side Electric Shuttle 


DRAFT
Climate Action Plan Update 
5-3 
5.2 Monitoring and Updates 
The CAP Update is intended to be a living document that provides flexibility for updates as new 
information becomes available and success is monitored over time. Understanding the success 
of the CAP Update requires a two-pronged approach, where success is viewed at the level of 
measures implementation status and compared against citywide GHG emissions trends over 
time. The City will begin implementing CAP Update measures and actions upon adoption, and 
data tracking will begin the following year. As 
measures are implemented, data on the timing and 
success 
of 
implementation, 
as 
well 
as 
communitywide GHG emissions trends, will be used 
to inform updates to individual GHG reduction 
measures and the CAP as a whole. The intent is to 
allow City staff to evaluate and monitor CAP 
performance over time and alter or amend the plan 
if it is not achieving the desired outcomes. Notable 
monitoring actions include regular tracking of the 
implementation status of each action, analysis of 
KPIs for reduction measures, and completing 
regular GHG emissions inventory updates. 
IMPLEMENTATION STATUS MONITORING 
City staff will annually evaluate the implementation status and performance of each GHG 
reduction measure in the CAP Update. This entails tracking if actions are being implemented on 
their intended timeline and monitoring the status of implementation for each. As measures are 
implemented, the KPI(s) associated with each measure will be monitored to understand if the 
measure is being successfully adopted in the community and achieving the intended GHG 
reductions. By evaluating whether the implementation of an action is on track to achieve its 
objective, the City can identify successful measures and actions and reevaluate or replace 
underperforming measures.  
GREENHOUSE GAS EMISSIONS INVENTORY UPDATES 
GHG emissions inventory updates will be regularly completed such that emissions trends over 
time can be compared for individual sectors and sources of emissions in the city. Currently, the 
San Diego Association of Governments (SANDAG) is leading the Regional Climate Action 
Planning Framework (ReCAP) program to provide cities with biennial GHG emissions 
Monitoring and Reporting will consist of: 
• 
Annual progress reports on 
implementation status of measures 
and actions 
• 
Annual updates to City Council and 
Commissions on progress and 
upcoming priorities 
• 
Biennial GHG inventory updates 
• 
Updates to the CAP every five years 


DRAFT
Oceanside General Plan Update 
5-4 
inventories. As these GHG inventories are provided to the City, City staff will compare the 
changes in emissions to the expected CAP Update GHG emissions reductions from reduction 
measures by sector, and progress towards meeting the overall GHG reduction targets. The City 
will also work with SANDAG to ensure that the GHG emissions inventories provide “apples-to-
apples” comparisons between years and are consistent with the methods used to develop the 
2018 baseline GHG emissions inventory included in this CAP Update. In such a case that the 
SANDAG ReCAP program is discontinued, the City will engage in developing its own GHG 
emissions inventories on a biennial basis after adoption of the CAP Update. 
IMPLEMENTATION STATUS REPORTING 
City staff will annually prepare a monitoring report that will provide updates on CAP 
implementation progress, including the implementation status of actions, progress on reaching 
KPIs for reduction measures, and GHG emissions inventory updates as they become available. 
The report would serve to provide transparency and promote engagement with the public for 
CAP implementation. The report will also be used to inform annual presentations to the City 
Council and relevant commissions; including the Planning Commission, Utilities Commission, 
and Parks and Recreation Commission; about implementation progress on actions and overall 
progress towards CAP objectives. As technologies and markets change and the City implements 
the actions in the CAP, these reports will be used to track progress and identify actions that need 
to be improved, adjusted, or replaced. 
Finally, the City will prepare an update to the CAP every 5 years, beginning in 2029. Updates 
would reflect the findings and recommendations of the monitoring reports and inventory 
updates. Future updates are necessary to account for any new state or federal legislation, market 
trends, or regional initiatives that may change how climate change mitigation is addressed at the 
local level. The updates also provide an opportunity to reassess measures and actions that may 
have been difficult to implement previously due to a lack of available technologies or high 
upfront implementation costs. 
FUTURE MEASURES AND ACTIONS FOR CONSIDERATION 
As the City of Oceanside monitors implementation progress of the GHG reduction measures of 
the CAP Update, it may be necessary to update GHG reduction measures to be more stringent in 
order to meet GHG reduction targets. Throughout the development of this CAP Update, the City 
considered a multitude of reduction measures to reach its GHG reduction targets, with making 
progress towards a 2045 reduction target being the most challenging due to the scale of change 
needed. The measures selected for inclusion in the CAP Update balanced many competing factors 
in what would make for successful and feasible implementation. Two primary factors included 


DRAFT
Climate Action Plan Update 
5-5 
the availability of resources for the City to develop and implement new policies and programs 
and the cost-effectiveness of new requirements for residents and businesses. As such, there are 
potential GHG reduction measures that could result in strong GHG reductions that were not 
included in the CAP Update, but may be considered for inclusion if measures are not achieving 
the projected GHG reductions. 
Presented below is a selection of GHG reduction measures that were considered for inclusion in 
the CAP Update but were not seen as feasibly implementable due to limited cost-effectiveness or 
the limited ability of the City to allocate resources for implementation. These measures are being 
documented here so that the City may consider them for future CAP Updates, or if the City is 
determined to not be on track for meeting GHG reduction targets. The City may consider other 
actions, such as impact fee programs to supplement resources to implement the CAP based on 
funding constraints and other impacts.  
• 
Develop a not-for-profit transportation 
demand management (TDM) agency to 
assist employers in the development and 
implementation of TDM plans for 
commute trips reductions. With 
development of this agency, require all 
existing and new employers that generate 
over 100 vehicle trips per day to join the 
agency, paying a membership fee that 
helps fund local TDM programs that are 
available to members.  
• 
Develop a nonresidential building energy benchmarking program that requires 
nonresidential buildings over a certain size to annually report energy consumption to the 
City and reduce energy consumption to below a specified benchmark.  
• 
Implement a reach code for new construction that sets a GHG emissions efficiency 
threshold.

5.3 California Environmental Quality Act and Local 
Climate Mitigation Efforts 
The implementation of the CAP Update will require that new developments are constructed with 
more sustainable and low-carbon features than existing development. New development 
projects consistent with the growth projections and applicable GHG reduction measures in the 
CAP are eligible for California Environmental Quality Act (CEQA) streamlining, per the 
provisions of State CEQA Guidelines Section 15183.5. Under these provisions, a project that is 
subject to discretionary review and is consistent with the City’s General Plan growth projections 
can show consistency with applicable GHG reduction measures in the CAP, and the level of 
analysis for the project’s GHG emissions required under CEQA can be streamlined. Furthermore, 
a project’s incremental contribution to cumulative GHG emissions may be determined not to be 
cumulatively considerable in such cases.  
The “qualified” CAP will allow project-specific environmental documents, if eligible, to tier from 
and/or incorporate by reference the CAP’s programmatic review of GHG impacts in their 
cumulative impact analyses. The CAP meets the requirements under Section 15183.5 of the State 
CEQA Guidelines as a qualified plan for the reduction of GHG emissions for use in cumulative 
impact analysis on new development projects. Details on how projects can demonstrate 
consistency with the CAP will be provided, with guidelines and a consistency review checklist. 
Note that the checklist is expected to be created within a year of CAP adoption.  
The consistency review checklist is the 
mechanism 
used 
by 
projects 
to 
demonstrate consistency with the CAP. The 
checklist contains GHG reduction measures 
applicable to development projects that are 
required to be implemented on a project-
by-project basis to ensure that the specified 
emissions targets identified in the CAP are 
achieved. New development projects will 
need to incorporate all applicable CAP 
measures to demonstrate consistency with 
the CAP. These measures will be enforced 
as conditions of approval to ensure that 
compliance can be confirmed before the 
project can be implemented.

Department to implement transit-supportive treatments on 25% of transit routes by 2045. 
The improvements can be a mix of roadway infrastructure improvements and/or traffic 
signal modifications that improve transit travel times and reliability. Treatments can 
include transit signal priority, bus-only signal phases, queue jumps, curb extensions to 
speed passenger loading, and dedicated bus lanes. The improvements would be planned 
and implemented in close coordination with NCTD, and the City would advocate for cost 
sharing among the agencies. 
Public Works 
(Traffic Engineering 
Division)

challenges for Oceanside residents and workers.

Action CS-1.2 
Support proposed GP policy RC 2-76 by requiring project proponents to compensate for 
tree removal during construction. Prior to the removal of any trees, proponents of 
projects shall obtain a tree removal permit, and if necessary, develop a tree avoidance, 
minimization, and replacement plan in consultation with a certified arborist or the City of 
Oceanside’s Department of Public Works. Replacement plantings should be native species 
where practicable. Invasive species (as defined by the California Invasive Plant Council) 
shall not be planted.

Subject: Greenhouse Gas Emissions Forecast and Targets Memorandum for the City of Oceanside Climate 
Action Plan Update  
 
 
INTRODUCTION 
The City of Oceanside (City) is currently in the process of updating its Climate Action Plan (CAP), which was first 
adopted in 2019, to provide a programmatic framework for the City to reduce its greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions in 
alignment with State legislation.1 The CAP Update includes a revision of the City’s baseline GHG emissions inventory, 
a forecast of future GHG emissions to align with growth projections of the in-progress General Plan Update, and an 
assessment of the GHG emissions reductions that will be needed to meet State GHG reduction targets. This 
memorandum summarizes the results of an updated GHG emissions forecast that aligns with the growth projects of 
the concurrent General Plan Update, and updated GHG reduction targets consistent with Senate Bill 32 (SB 32) and 
Assembly Bill 1279 (AB 1279).  
The changes to the GHG emissions context for the CAP Update included in this memorandum allow for more 
streamlined monitoring of citywide emissions levels over time. The forecasts also account for the reduction impact of 
State regulations that have been adopted since the 2019 CAP was developed. The 2019 CAP utilized a baseline year 
of 2013 for GHG emission inventory, which is being updated to a 2018 baseline year in the CAP Update. Since the 
2013 inventory was developed, San Diego Association of Governments (SANDAG) has updated its travel demand 
model utilized for vehicle miles traveled (VMT) modeling and has started providing cities within San Diego county 
with GHG emissions inventories at no cost through the Regional Climate Action Planning Framework (ReCAP) 
Snapshot program. Updating the baseline from 2013 to 2018 allows the City to regularly track GHG emissions with 
consistent data sources and methods through GHG emissions inventories provided by SANDAG. Additionally, several 
state-level regulations have been adopted that provide additional GHG reductions in the City of Oceanside and are 
accounted for in the GHG emissions forecast. These include updated California Building Energy Efficiency Standards 
and the Advanced Clean Cars II regulations. The GHG reduction impact of the City joining the Clean Energy Alliance 
(CEA), to automatically enroll electricity accounts throughout Oceanside at a 50 percent renewable electricity option, 
is also considered as part of the GHG emissions forecast for the CAP Update.  
The purpose of this CAP Update is to develop near- and long-term strategies for reducing GHG emissions in line with 
State targets. As such, the GHG reduction targets of the CAP Update are being adjusted to align with the statewide 
2030 target established by SB 32 and the 2045 target established by AB 1279. Additionally, an updated method for 
target setting is being utilized given the legal precedence established by California Supreme Court decision in Center 
for Biological Diversity v. California Department of Fish and Wildlife and Newhall Land and Farming (2015) 62 Cal.4th 
 
1 Assembly Bill 1279 was signed into law in 2022, which formally adopted a long-term statewide target for reducing GHG emissions to net-zero before 2045, 
including an 85% reduction in anthropogenic GHG emissions below 1990 levels before 2045. 


City of Oceanside CAP Update 
GHG Emissions Forecasts and Targets Report 
December 18, 2023 
Page 2 
 
204, and the recommendations of the California Association of Environmental Professionals’ Final White Paper Beyond 
2020 and Newhall (AEP 2016). With this, it is recommended that GHG reduction targets are established on a mass 
emissions basis (as compared to per capita emissions) and that a sector-based approach is taken, where targets are 
established based on the State’s reduction goals for locally specific economic sectors. For example, the State’s targets 
for reducing emission in the “Industrial” economic sector, are not considered for Oceanside’s GHG reduction target, 
as facilities that fall under this State categorization do not exist in the city. This allows for the CAP Update to only 
consider relevant emissions sources in its GHG reduction targets, and provides transparency in how citywide 
emissions will be reduced in line with State legislation. 
This memorandum summarizes the results of the updated GHG emissions context for the CAP Update, with a 
detailed explanation of the methods and assumptions included in Attachment A GHG Emissions Forecast and Targets 
Technical Documentation. 
ORGANIZATION OF THIS MEMORANDUM 
This memorandum consists of four parts:  
 
Section 1: Greenhouse Gas Emissions Inventory presents an overview of the City’s 2018 communitywide GHG 
emissions inventory. 
 
Section 2: Updated Greenhouse Gas Emissions Forecasts summarizes the forecasted GHG emissions under 
“business-as-usual” (BAU) and Adjusted BAU (ABAU) scenarios for years 2030, 2045, and 2050.  
 
Section 3: Updated Greenhouse Gas Reduction Targets describes the approach for establishing targets for CAP 
Update.  
 
Section 4: Analysis of Updated Targets Attainment presents the analysis conducted for identifying the emissions 
gap that the City needs to address to achieve the CAP Update targets.  
1 
GREENHOUSE GAS EMISSIONS INVENTORY 
The GHG emissions inventory provides the existing conditions for the CAP Update, serving to identify the 
contribution of different communitywide GHG emissions sources in Oceanside, and establish a baseline for 
forecasting future emissions, setting GHG reduction targets, and monitoring progress towards reaching the target 
over time. The GHG emissions inventory presented here is for calendar year 2018, which was provided by SANDAG 
through the ReCAP Snapshot program (SANDAG 2023).2 An updated GHG emissions inventory is being used to 
provide a baseline for forecasting future emissions from the most recently available data, and a consistent baseline 
for future emissions inventories that are expected to be provided by SANDAG on an ongoing basis. While previous 
GHG inventories have been completed for Oceanside, including a 2013 inventory for the 2019 CAP, use of the 
updated baseline for 2018 allows for consistent comparison of emissions over time with the data sources and 
methods used by SANDAG. Consistent emissions tracking is essential to verify the effectiveness of program and 
policy implementation for the CAP Update. With use of an updated emissions inventory baseline, the GHG emissions 
forecast, and reduction targets will also be updated from those included in the 2019 CAP. 
Some modifications to the 2018 GHG emissions inventory provided through the ReCAP program have been made to 
provide consistency with GHG emissions forecasting methods and to encompass relevant emissions sources in 
Oceanside. Because the CAP Update is being developed as part of the General Plan Update which includes an 
updated land use scenario, VMT modeling was completed to refine future VMT estimates to be consistent with 
anticipated growth patterns in Oceanside. To align with the VMT modeling completed for the General Plan Update, 
 
2  The GHG emissions inventory provided through ReCAP is a “communitywide” emissions inventory, which covers all relevant GHG emissions sources in the 
City of Oceanside boundary. Municipal government operations are considered within this communitywide inventory, but are not explicitly disaggregated.

the VMT data used to calculate on-road transportation emissions was updated from that used in the 2018 inventory 
as provided by ReCAP Snapshot. The VMT data provide through ReCAP utilizes the SANDAG ABM2+ model version 
14.0.1; whereas the VMT modeling consistent with the General Plan Update land use scenario uses the SANDAG 
ABM2+ model version 14.3.0, resulting an approximately 2 percent lower VMT total for Oceanside for the year 2018 
than provided through ReCAP. Additionally, the 2018 emissions inventory as provided through ReCAP did not include 
estimates from off-road equipment use, which have been added to the GHG inventory presented here.  
1.1 
2018 GHG EMISSIONS INVENTORY RESULTS 
The results of the 2018 communitywide GHG emissions inventory demonstrate the contribution of different GHG 
emissions sources in Oceanside by emissions sector. The largest contributor to total emissions was fuel combustion in 
on-road vehicles, representing 51 percent of total emissions. The next two largest emissions sources were grid-
purchased electricity and natural gas use in buildings and facilities within the city, contributing 22 percent and 18 
percent to total emissions, respectively. The remaining emissions sectors represent approximately 9 percent of total 
emissions, including solid waste, off-road transportation, water, and wastewater. In total, communitywide emissions 
sources considered in the 2018 emissions inventory generated approximately 846,000 metric tons of carbon dioxide 
equivalent (MTCO2e). These results are presented in Figure 1 and Table 1, showing the total emissions by sector with 
the representative contribution to total communitywide emissions.

2 
UPDATED GREENHOUSE GAS EMISSIONS FORECASTS 
The GHG emissions forecast estimates how communitywide GHG emissions for Oceanside may grow given housing 
and economic growth, and how state and federal regulations may help to reduce local emissions. Developing a GHG 
emissions forecast is an essential step for the CAP, as it will provide insight into what future emissions levels may be, 
and necessary type and scale of actions that will be needed in each GHG emissions sector to reduce emissions within 
local control for State GHG reduction target alignment. As such, a GHG emissions forecast is presented as a sector-
level assessment of near-term (2030) and long-term (2045 and 2050) GHG emissions projections based on current 
conditions under two scenarios. Under the first scenario, GHG emissions would grow from 2018 levels at the same 
rates as housing, population, employment, and vehicle travel, which is known as a business-as-usual scenario (BAU) 
forecast. The BAU forecast serves as a basis for understanding how emissions levels may change with growth, and 
how far GHG emissions will need to be reduced in future years to meet GHG reduction targets without considering 
any future action to reduce GHG emissions. The second scenario considers the GHG reduction impact of local, state, 
and federal regulations, which is known as a legislative adjusted business-as-usual scenario (ABAU) forecast. The 
ABAU forecast provides a more detailed assessment of future GHG emissions by showing how currently adopted 
local, state and federal regulations will help Oceanside meet its GHG reduction targets. The future years selected for 
the GHG emissions forecasts are aligned with the GHG reduction target years for State legislation, 2030 for SB 32 and 
2045 for AB 1279, as well as 2050 to align with the General Plan Update horizon year. 
The BAU and ABAU forecasts presented in this memorandum for the CAP Update are updated from those in the 2019 
CAP to incorporate the best available data and to be consistent with the General Plan Update. The updated BAU 
forecast utilizes growth projections that are consistent with the land use scenarios included in the General Plan 
Update and is based on the 2018 GHG emissions inventory, as compared to 2013 in the 2019 CAP. Additionally, the 
ABAU forecast incorporates new State regulations that were not in place during development of the 2019 CAP, such 
On-Road Transportation

as 2022 California Building Energy Efficiency Standards and Advanced Clean Cars II regulations. As such, the CAP 
Update will be based on a more likely future emissions scenario, given an ever-evolving local GHG emission profile 
and the reduction efforts at the state and federal level. 
Since development of the 2019 CAP, the City of Oceanside has committed to joining the CEA, in which electricity 
accounts in Oceanside will be automatically enrolled at the 50 percent renewable electricity option starting in 2024. 
Since this is a committed local policy that will be officially implemented before the CAP Update is adopted, the GHG 
reduction impact of the City joining CEA is also considered as part of the ABAU forecast.  
2.1 
BUSINESS-AS-USUAL SCENARIO GREENHOUSE GAS EMISSIONS 
FORECAST  
The BAU forecast was developed by scaling communitywide GHG emissions from the 2018 emissions inventory by the 
appropriate growth scaling factors. The growth scaling factors used include population, housing units, and number of 
jobs; and were applied to various emissions sources based on their relationship to the GHG emissions source. For 
example, growth in residential building electricity consumption would be expected to scale with an increase in 
number of housing units, and commercial energy consumption would be expected to scale with the number of 
employees at businesses in Oceanside. More detail on the approach for applying growth scaling factors to specific 
GHG emissions sectors is provided in Attachment A. VMT for on-road transportation is also essential for 
understanding how future GHG emissions may change in Oceanside. VMT was modeled using the SANDAG ABM2+ 
model version 14.3.0, with adjustments made to account for the growth in housing units, population, and jobs 
included in the General Plan Update. The growth scaling factors and VMT used for the BAU forecast were obtained 
from the land use scenario included in the General Plan Update and are provided in Table 2.  
Table 2

impact of growth in the City.

2.2 
LEGISLATIVE ADJUSTED BUSINESS-AS-USUAL SCENARIO 
GREENHOUSE GAS EMISSIONS FORECAST  
The ABAU forecast was developed by accounting for the expected GHG reductions in communitywide GHG emissions 
in Oceanside that are expected to occur as a result of adopted local, state, and federal regulations. The GHG 
reductions considered in the ABAU forecast fall into three categories: building efficiency standards, fuel efficiency 
standards and electric vehicle sale requirements, and renewable electricity requirements. The full list of legislation 
considered in the ABAU forecast is provided in Attachment A. The ABAU scenario also considers the GHG reductions 
that are expected to be realized through the City joining CEA, to provide 50 percent renewable energy to electricity 
customers throughout the City starting in 2024. 
The results ABAU forecasts show that emissions are expected to decline from 2018 levels through 2050, as shown in

3 
UPDATED GREENHOUSE GAS REDUCTION TARGETS  
A primary purpose of the CAP Update is to align the City of Oceanside’s GHG reduction targets with the most recent 
legislation. Since the 2019 CAP was adopted, AB 1279 was signed into law, which requires the State to develop and 
implement a strategy for achieving statewide net-zero emissions by 2045 or sooner, which includes an 85 percent 
reduction in anthropogenic GHG emissions by 2045 with remaining emissions offset by carbon dioxide removal. With 
this, the CAP Update will include a long-term GHG reduction target in alignment with AB 1279. The 2030 GHG 
reduction target of the CAP update is maintaining consistency with the 2019 CAP by aligning with SB 32. However, 
the method for setting the 2030 reduction target is being updated to utilize a sector specific approach, which 
considers only the statewide reductions for specific GHG emissions sectors relevant to the City of Oceanside. 
Additionally, a 2050 target is being added to align with the General Plan Update horizon year. The updated targets 
for the CAP Update will provide the City with GHG reduction targets for the years 2030, 2045, and 2050 that utilize 
consistent approaches and align with the most recent State legislation. All GHG reduction targets have been 
translated to be measured from the baseline of the 2018 GHG emissions inventory.  
3.1 
ALIGNING WITH STATEWIDE TARGETS  
The primary statewide GHG reduction targets that are relevant to the CAP Update are those established by SB 32 of 
2016 and AB 1279 of 2022, which include: 


City of Oceanside CAP Update 
GHG Emissions Forecasts and Targets Report 
December 18, 2023 
Page 9 
 
 
Reducing statewide GHG emissions 40 percent below 1990 levels by 2030 (SB 32), and 
 
Reducing statewide anthropogenic GHG emissions 85 percent below 1990 levels before 2045 (AB 1279). 
With the State’s adoption of these legislative targets, the California Air Resources Board was directed to develop and 
implement strategies to reduce statewide emissions consistent with the targets through a Climate Change Scoping 
Plan (Scoping Plan). The Scoping Plan outlines the State’s strategy to reduce GHG emissions in each economic sector 
and is regularly updated to reflect the most recent trends, technology, and legislative reduction targets. The 2017 
Scoping Plan outlines the State’s strategy for achieving the 2030 reduction target of SB 32 (CARB 2017), and the 2022 
Scoping Plan builds upon this strategy to demonstrate how net-zero emissions will be achieved in 2045 or sooner 
consistent with AB 1279 (CARB 2022). Defensible GHG reduction targets at the local level must demonstrate 
alignment with statewide targets and the strategies outlined in the appropriate Scoping Plan. 
For the CAP Update to demonstrate alignment with the State’s legislatively adopted GHG reduction targets, the CAP 
Update must demonstrate an emissions trajectory supported by the State’s Scoping Plan. In the Scoping Plan, GHG 
reduction scenarios are modeled by GHG emission sectors for the State to achieve the corresponding target. The City 
of Oceanside can align GHG reduction targets in the CAP Update with the Scoping Plan’s GHG emission scenarios for 
only the GHG emissions sectors that are relevant to local conditions. For example, the State’s forecasts for reducing 
emission in the “Industrial” sector are not applicable to Oceanside’s GHG reduction target, as facilities that fall under 
this State categorization do not exist in the city. This target setting approach is consistent with the legal precedence 
established by the California Supreme Court decision in Center for Biological Diversity v. California Department of Fish 
and Wildlife and Newhall Land and Farming (2015) 62 Cal.4th 204 and the recommendations of the California 
Association of Environmental Professionals’ Final White Paper Beyond 2020 and Newhall (AEP 2016). More detail on 
the specific sectors relevant to the City of Oceanside is available in Attachment A. 
Basis for the 2030 GHG Reduction Target 
For 2030, the CAP Update’s target is aligned with the SB 32 and the statewide GHG emissions pathways towards 
reaching this target. The 2030 target for the CAP Update is consistent with a 40 percent reduction in statewide GHG 
emissions below 1990 levels, by applying the sector specific targets of relevant GHG emissions sectors to Oceanside’s 
emissions profile as outlined in the 2017 Scoping Plan. This reduction equivalent is translated to be measured from a 
2018 baseline, assuming that GHG emissions in the City have generally followed statewide emissions trends since 1990.  
Basis for the 2045 GHG Reduction Target 
For 2045, the CAP Update’s target is aligned with AB 1279 and the statewide GHG emissions pathways towards 
reaching this target, as outlined in the 2022 Scoping Plan. AB 1279 requires that the State’s net zero emissions target 
by 2045 be achieved by reducing statewide anthropogenic emissions4 by at least 85 percent below 1990 levels before 
2045. The 2045 target for the CAP Update is consistent with an 85 percent reduction in statewide anthropogenic 
GHG emissions below 1990 levels, by applying the sector specific targets of relevant GHG emissions sectors to 
Oceanside’s emissions profile as outlined in the 2022 Scoping Plan. This reduction equivalent is translated to be 
measured from a 2018 baseline, assuming that GHG emissions in the city have generally followed statewide emissions 
trends since 1990.  
 
4  Anthropogenic emissions include the primary sources and activities within the City’s GHG emissions sectors: on-road transportation, electricity, natural gas, 
solid waste, off-road transportation, water, and wastewater. 


City of Oceanside CAP Update 
GHG Emissions Forecasts and Targets Report 
December 18, 2023 
Page 10 
 
Basis for the 2050 GHG Reduction Target 
For 2050, the CAP Update’s GHG reduction target is consistent with the 2045 target, such that GHG emissions cannot 
increase above the 2045 target levels.

4 
ANALYSIS OF UPDATED TARGETS ATTAINMENT 
The purpose of the CAP Update is to develop strategies the City can implement to reduce communitywide GHG 
emissions consistent with local reduction targets. With the GHG emissions forecast and updated reduction targets 
presented in this memorandum, the City can assess the GHG reductions it will need to achieve to meet the reduction 
targets, or bridge the “emissions gap.” The emissions gap analysis demonstrates the future expected GHG emissions 
levels from the ABAU forecast for each GHG emissions sector, and additional GHG reductions that will need to be 
achieved by the reduction strategies of the CAP Update to align with State GHG reduction targets.  
4.1 
EMISSIONS GAP ANALYSIS 
Comparing the City’s projected GHG emissions levels from the ABAU forecast with the updated reduction targets 
demonstrates that additional emissions reductions through local action in the CAP Update are necessary for the City 
to achieve its targets. Table 7 provides a summary of the ABAU forecast in comparison with the updated GHG 
reduction targets, and the emissions gap that would need to be reduced to meet the targets.

4.2 
MEETING THE TARGETS 
With locally specific GHG reduction targets developed and an understanding of the future emissions gap, the next 
steps are to develop GHG reduction strategies to meet the targets through the CAP Update. The GHG reduction 
strategies of the CAP Update will build upon the successes of the 2019 CAP and provide a pathway for reaching the 
deep GHG reductions needed to align with State GHG reduction targets.

‐
                           
1) Update the OFFROAD2021 tab with latest run for jurisidiction in question. 
2) Enter the jurisdiction scaling method for each sub‐sector. Choose from the drop down cell. For sub‐sectors that are not active in the jurisdiction, leave the jurisdiction scaling method blank.
3) Emissions will automatically be calculated from the OFFROAD fuel outputs based on the jurisdiction scaling method chosen. 
4) Do not add or remove any rows that are already in the table below. The rows have been prepopulated with all possible sub‐sectors and fuel types. Only adjust the jurisdiction scaling method and leave blank, if not 
applicable.
• Enter Inventory Data into the colored cells starting in row 4. Grey cells are for calculations only.
• Insert rows within the table as needed. 
• Only input data in the colored cells.
• See comments in headers for specific input instructions.

Sonam Sahu, Andrew Beecher, Brenda Hom, Poonam Boparai  
Subject: GHG Reduction Measures Technical Memorandum for the City of Oceanside Climate Action Plan 
Update 
 
 
INTRODUCTION 
The City of Oceanside (City) is currently in the process of updating its Climate Action Plan (CAP), which was first 
adopted in 2019, to provide a programmatic framework for the City to reduce its greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions in 
alignment with State legislation.1 The CAP Update includes the development of GHG reduction measures that will set 
the City on a pathway towards alignment with the GHG reduction targets established by Senate Bill 32 (SB 32) and 
Assembly Bill 1279 (AB 1279). An updated GHG emissions forecast has been completed, which estimates how future 
emissions may evolve as growth in the City continues for the years 2030, 2045, and 2050, as provided in the 
Greenhouse Gas Emissions Forecast and Targets Memorandum for the City of Oceanside Climate Action Plan Update 
(GHG Forecast and Targets Memo). The GHG emissions forecast presents two future scenarios, a business-as-usual 
scenario (BAU) and a legislative adjusted business-as-usual scenario (ABAU). The BAU forecast demonstrates how GHG 
emissions in the City would increase with growth in housing units, population, and jobs; while the ABAU forecast 
demonstrates how future GHG emissions in the BAU forecast would be expected to change when accounting for the 
GHG reduction impact of State, Federal, and local legislation and programs. The GHG Forecast and Targets Memo 
also outlines GHG reduction targets for the CAP Update that would align with the State legislative targets under SB 
32 and AB 1279. Comparing the results of the ABAU forecast with the GHG reduction targets shows that the City 
would need to implement additional policies and programs to further reduce GHG emissions in Oceanside to align 
with State legislative targets in the CAP Update.  
A suite of GHG reduction measures have been developed for inclusion in the CAP Update that can be feasibly 
implemented by the City to reduce GHG emissions in alignment with State legislative targets. Each GHG reduction 
measure is categorized under a GHG emissions sector for which the measure’s implementation would reduce GHG 
emissions. These “sectors” do not always directly align with those presented in the City’s GHG emissions inventory, as 
some measures are cross-cutting in nature, while others are intended to provide additional community benefits while 
reducing emissions from sources not accounted for in the City’s GHG emissions inventory.2 Each GHG reduction 
measure also includes implementing actions that provide discrete steps for the City to take in successfully meeting 
 
1 Assembly Bill 1279 was signed into law in 2022, which formally adopted a long-term statewide target for reducing GHG emissions to net-zero before 2045, 
including an 85% reduction in anthropogenic GHG emissions below 1990 levels before 2045. 
2 The primary sectors by which measures are organized that do not align with the City’s GHG emissions inventory sectors are Carbon Sequestration and Local 
Green Economy. Carbon Sequestration is not accounted for in the City’s current inventory, as a comprehensive study on the amount of carbon sequestered in 
natural and agricultural lands and the urban forest is not currently available for Oceanside. The Local Green Economy sectors is also not accounted for, as it is 
intended to reduce emissions associated with the consumption of consumer products, by which GHG emissions are emitted outside of Oceanside’s boundary or 
control across the globe throughout product supply chains. 


Oceanside CAP Update GHG Reductions Memo 
January 16, 2024 
Page 2 
 
the goals of measures. These “actions” have been developed to outline sufficient detail for both stakeholders and City 
departments tasked with implementation to have transparency in the process of the City’s transition towards a low 
carbon future.  
This memorandum presents the GHG reduction measures and implementing actions for consideration in the City’s 
CAP update, with the quantified GHG reduction potential for each measure. The measures and actions have been 
developed in coordination with City staff and are based on measures identified in the 2019 CAP, the in-progress 
General Plan Update, as well as new measures and actions informed by current best practices. The measures and 
actions strive to balance the need for deep GHG reductions to meet State aligned targets, the availability of resources 
for the City to implement new programs, and equity considerations. Details on the calculation of GHG reductions 
associated with each measure are provided in Attachment A GHG Reduction Potential Technical Documentation. 
ORGANIZATION OF THIS MEMORANDUM 
This memorandum consists of four parts:  
 
Section 1: Greenhouse Gas Emissions Forecast, Targets, and Gap Analysis Summary describes the results of the 
ABAU forecast, recommended GHG reduction targets, and the “emissions gap” that would need to be met with 
GHG reduction measures in the CAP Update, as detailed in the Forecast and Targets Memo. 
 
Section 2: Greenhouse Gas Reduction Measures Summary provides an overview of the GHG reduction measures 
considered for the CAP Update and their calculated GHG reduction potential.  
 
Section 3: Greenhouse Gas Reduction Measures Detail outlines each GHG reduction measure, and the 
implementing actions associated with each measure.  
GREENHOUSE GAS EMISSIONS FORECAST, TARGETS,  
AND GAP ANALYSIS SUMMARY 
The results of the ABAU GHG emissions forecast, reduction targets, and gap analysis, as presented in the Forecast 
and Targets Memo, are summarized here to provide the GHG emissions context for the reduction measures 
considered for the CAP Update. The ABAU forecast shows how emissions are expected to change in the City of 
Oceanside for the years 2030, 2045, and 2050, considering the growth projections of the in-progress General Plan 
Update, and the impacts of State and Federal legislation on local GHG emissions. The impact of the City joining the 
Clean Energy Alliance is also considered in the ABAU forecast. The GHG reduction targets are aligned with SB 32 and 
AB 1279 statewide GHG reduction targets, which include: 
 
Reducing statewide GHG emissions 40 percent below 1990 levels by 2030 (SB 32), and 
 
Reducing statewide anthropogenic GHG emissions 85 percent below 1990 levels no later than 2045 (AB 1279). 
These statewide targets were converted into local targets for the CAP Update by adjusting the baseline from 1990 to 
2018 and reduction percentages based on the State’s sector-level analysis of past and future GHG emissions. The gap 
analysis is done by comparing the results of the ABAU forecast and the local targets for the CAP Update to 
understand the magnitude of emissions reductions that will need to be achieved through measures in the CAP 
Update. This “emissions gap” is key for determining the actions that will be needed to address GHG emissions in each 
sector. 
The ABAU forecast, reduction targets, and gap analysis are summarized in Table 1. The results show that GHG 
emissions are expected to decrease below 2018 levels through 2045; however, an emissions gap still remains between 
emissions reduction under the ABAU scenario and future emissions levels consistent with state legislative reduction 


Oceanside CAP Update GHG Reductions Memo 
January 16, 2024 
Page 3 
 
targets. Based on the results of the ABAU forecast, GHG emissions reduction measures can be targeted towards the 
GHG emissions sectors that contribute most to citywide emissions, including natural gas, waste, on-road 
transportation, and off-road transportation.

GREENHOUSE GAS REDUCTION MEASURES SUMMARY 
The GHG reduction measures presented below are organized under six focus sectors that generally align with 
emissions sectors considered in the 2018 GHG emissions inventory, including: 
 
 Energy, which includes measures that reduce emissions in the natural gas and electricity GHG inventory sectors;  
 
Transportation, which includes measures that reduce emissions in the on-road transportation and off-road 
transportation GHG inventory sectors; 
 
Solid Waste;  
 
Water;  
 
Carbon Sequestration, which reduces the City’s overall GHG emissions contribution; and  
 
Local Green Economy, which is intended to reduce upstream emissions not accounted for in the GHG inventory.  
Each sector includes one or more GHG reduction measures, and each measure includes multiple implementation 
actions. Implementation actions include one or more key actions that drive quantifiable GHG reductions, as well as 
multiple supportive actions that support effective implementation of the measures and key actions. 
Performance indicators are provided for measures that have quantifiable GHG reductions to allow the City to monitor 
progress towards meeting the GHG reduction goal of the measure as the actions of the measure are implemented. 


Oceanside CAP Update GHG Reductions Memo 
January 16, 2024 
Page 4 
 
Implementation progress for reduction measures that do not include performance indicators can be monitored 
based on whether the implementation actions have been completed.  
GHG reductions associated with the measures were calculated in a stepwise manner for the future years 2030, 2045, 
and 2050. In other words, GHG reductions (in MTCO2e) are assessed during a snapshot in time in years 2030, 2045, 
and 2050. Measures are quantified for a single year rather than adding cumulative reductions from prior years, which 
aligns with the methodology used to develop the City’s GHG reduction targets. Importantly, GHG emissions 
reductions were quantified for measures wherever substantial evidence and reasonable assumptions were available 
to support calculations. Some of the GHG reduction measures were identified as not quantifiable because of lack of 
available data or quantification methods but would still be expected to reduce GHG emissions. These measures are 
listed in this technical memorandum and will be discussed qualitatively in the CAP Update. They can be monitored for 
potential quantification opportunities in the future if data and/or quantification methods become available.  
Estimates of GHG emissions reductions, along with an estimated emissions “gap” (i.e., the difference between GHG 
reductions required to meet the targets and total estimated GHG reductions), are summarized in Table 2. 
Descriptions of the measures are provided in the following sections. Detailed measure calculations and assumptions 
supporting the GHG reduction estimates are provided in Attachment A.

GREENHOUSE GAS REDUCTION MEASURES DETAIL 
The following section provides details on the intent and structure of each GHG reduction measure for consideration 
in the CAP Update, as well as additional information about the quantification of GHG reductions associated with the 
measure. Each measure includes a measure summary, which provides an overview of how the implementing actions 


Oceanside CAP Update GHG Reductions Memo 
January 16, 2024 
Page 6 
 
support GHG reductions and successful implementation of the measure. A brief description of the approach for 
quantifying GHG reductions with the measure is also provided, highlighting key data and assumptions, with further 
detail on calculations provided in Attachment A. Lastly, the full set of implementation actions for each GHG reduction 
measure is provided in a table, with the quantified GHG reductions. The actions are noted as “key” or “supportive” to 
highlight which actions are intended to create effective change in activities that generate GHG emissions and which 
actions are supportive to the key actions and the overall successful implementation of the measure. Additionally, 
actions that support equity and the allocation of the benefits of programs to all residents are noted as “inclusive”.  
ENERGY 
GHG emissions from energy consumption and generation represented 39% of the City’s total emissions in 2018. Even 
as electricity-related emissions are reduced through procurement of renewable electricity from joining Clean Energy 
Alliance, natural gas consumption will continue to represent a significant proportion of the City’s total GHG emissions 
through 2045 and 2050. The GHG reduction measures and actions included in the Energy sector strive to promote 
energy efficiency, increase renewable energy availability, and reduce future consumption of natural gas. 
Measure E-1: Increase renewable energy availability by providing 100% renewable electricity 
through the Clean Energy Alliance (CEA) by 2030 with a 95% participation rate. 
MEASURE SUMMARY 
To reduce emissions associated with electricity, the City plans to increase the availability of renewable energy to 
community members. The City has already made significant progress by joining the Clean Energy Alliance (CEA), a 
community choice aggregation (CCA) program, providing residents and businesses with 50% renewable electricity as 
a default for those who do not opt-out as of January 2024. The City can further the impact of joining CEA by 
purchasing 100% renewable electricity and increasing the default electricity rate to 100% renewable electricity for 
accounts in the community. Regular outreach campaigns will be conducted to encourage continued participation in 
CEA, which includes sharing information online and promoting CEA participation at community events.  
GHG QUANTIFICATION APPROACH 
The quantification of GHG reductions for Measure E-1 assumes a reduction in the emissions intensity of the electricity 
supply for Oceanside. It is assumed 95% of all electricity consumption is 100% in 2030, resulting in GHG reductions 
beyond the statewide minimum Renewables Portfolio Standard (RPS) requirements for GHG-free electricity. In 2045 
and 2050, no additional reductions are seen from implementation of this measure, as all retail electricity in the state is 
expected to be 100% GHG-free, consistent with RPS minimum requirements. Double counting of emissions 
reductions from other energy related measures was avoided by accounting for the reduction in grid-supplied 
electricity from increased solar photovoltaic (PV) om Measures E-2 and increased energy efficiency in Measure E-3 
prior to accounting for the shift to more GHG-free electricity.  
Identifier 
Measure/Action 
GHG Reduction Potential (MTCO2e)

Action E-1.1 
Provide renewable energy as a default to all electricity accounts in the community through CEA at 50% renewables mix rate 
by 2025 at the time of enrollment. (Completed) 
Action E-1.2 
Enroll 100% of City accounts in 100% renewable/zero-carbon option from CEA by 2030. (Key) 


Oceanside CAP Update GHG Reductions Memo 
January 16, 2024 
Page 7 
 
Action E-1.3 
Update the agreement with CEA to increase the default for all CEA customers in the community to 100% renewable energy 
by 2030. (Key) 
Action E-1.4 
Annually track CEA participation rates, including opt-out and opt-down trends, and work with CEA to develop strategies for 
maintaining or increasing participation. (Supportive) 
Action E-1.5 
Conduct annual community engagement campaigns to promote CEA renewable energy choices and programs through print, 
social media and digital media through Green Oceanside sustainability campaigns and community events. (Supportive) 
Measure E-2: Increase behind-the-meter solar photovoltaic (PV) capacity in the City to 125 
megawatts (MW) by 2030 and 165 MW by 2045. 
MEASURE SUMMARY 
To enhance the resilience of the city's energy infrastructure and reduce reliance on grid supplied electricity, the City 
plans to increase the availability of local renewable energy in Oceanside. Through incremental expansion of the PV 
capacity at both City and privately-owned properties, the City plans to achieve at least 125 MW of communitywide 
solar capacity by 2030 and 165 MW by 2045. The City will ensure installation of solar infrastructure through adopting 
building code standards that require solar on all new construction and installing solar arrays at City-owned facilities. 
The City will also support the voluntary installation of solar infrastructure through expediting the permitting process, 
promoting financing options, and partnering with CEA to support installation of solar at low-income households.  
GHG QUANTIFICATION APPROACH 
The quantification of GHG reductions for Measure E-2 is based on the shift of grid supplied electricity to GHG-free 
energy generated on-site through solar PV. The reduction quantification assumes that the targets of 125 MW citywide 
solar PV capacity by 2030 and 165 MW capacity by 2045 can feasibly be met through building code standards and 
direct installation by the City, beyond the 78 MW capacity as of 2020 (SANDAG 2023). The additional installation does 
not account for voluntary installation of solar on private property. Recent changes in the cost-effectiveness of solar 
installation due to regulatory changes in the net-metering rate structure for solar PV make it difficult to estimate 
future voluntary installations based on past trends. Double counting of emissions reductions with Measure E-1 was 
avoided by accounting for the grid demand reduction from Measure E-2 through shifting to onsite renewables, prior 
to adjusting for GHG-free grid supplied electricity.
